Part 1 · Big Picture

01 · What is Agent Framework

Microsoft Agent Framework (MAF) is an open-source framework written in Python and .NET that connects large language models (LLMs) to real apps and orchestrates them into useful agents and multi-agent workflows. It does not train models — it owns all the plumbing around the model, and it is built for production.

🔌 Analogy
Think of an LLM as a powerful but isolated engine: it emits text, but knows nothing about your database, can't call tools by itself, and every vendor's interface is different. MAF is the standardized "drivetrain + dashboard + connector kit" — drive any engine with one codebase, wire it to tools, memory and other agents, and monitor, restart and gate it in production.

What does it actually solve?

Calling a vendor SDK directly works, but real apps hit these pain points fast — exactly what MAF smooths over:

PainWithout a frameworkWhat MAF does
Vendor lock-inSwitching models rewrites a lot of codeOne ChatClient + Agent; switching vendors is ~one line
Assembling chatHand-build role/content dictsStructured Message / Role / Content
Calling toolsParse which function & args yourself@tool auto-generates schema + parses + executes
Multi-step / multi-agentHand-write the think–act loop & coordinationAgent loop + graph-based Workflows
Going to productionRoll your own tracing, persistence, approvalsBuilt-in OpenTelemetry, checkpoints, human-in-the-loop, hosting

What the smallest agent looks like

samples/01-get-started/01_hello_agent.pyminimal
from agent_framework import Agent
from agent_framework.foundry import FoundryChatClient
from azure.identity import AzureCliCredential

client = FoundryChatClient(
    project_endpoint="https://your-project.services.ai.azure.com",
    model="gpt-4o", credential=AzureCliCredential(),
)
agent = Agent(
    client=client, name="HelloAgent",
    instructions="You are a friendly assistant. Keep your answers brief.",
)

result = await agent.run("What is the capital of France?")
print(result)

Three steps: pick a ChatClient (connect to a model) → wrap it in an Agent (name + system instructions) → run a prompt. Switch vendors by replacing FoundryChatClient with OpenAIChatClient — nothing else changes.

The core mental model

Your code
input + instructions
→
Agent
assemble / loop
→
ChatClient
vendor-agnostic
→
LLM
+ tools / memory
1 How does it relate to LangChain / AutoGen / Semantic Kernel? expand
❓ One-line positioning
MAF is Microsoft's convergence of Semantic Kernel (enterprise, strongly typed, rich connectors) and AutoGen (multi-agent conversations & research), into one framework aimed at prototype-to-production, with aligned Python and .NET implementations.
✅ Its trade-offs
It wants both LangGraph-style graph orchestration (controllable, resumable) and AutoGen-style multi-agent collaboration, while making observability / persistence / approvals first-class — that's what "production-grade" means.
2 How do you escape vendor lock-in? expand
🧪 Example
Each SDK looks different: 
# OpenAI
from openai import OpenAI
client = OpenAI()
r = client.chat.completions.create(model="gpt-4o", messages=[...])

# Anthropic
from anthropic import Anthropic
client = Anthropic()
r = client.messages.create(model="claude-3-7-sonnet", max_tokens=1024, messages=[...])
With MAF only the client swaps; the rest stays: 
from agent_framework import Agent
from agent_framework.openai import OpenAIChatClient
# from agent_framework.anthropic import AnthropicClient  # only this line changes
agent = Agent(client=OpenAIChatClient(model="gpt-4o"), instructions="…")
await agent.run("hi")
❓ Why this matters
Three very real reasons:
① cost — pricing for the same task differs 5–20× across models;
② fast model turnover — a new SOTA arrives every quarter; lock-in means missing it;
③ compliance & availability — finance/gov demand private deployment; some regions need Foundry, others a corporate gateway.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF abstracts every vendor into a uniform ChatClient interface. FoundryChatClient, OpenAIChatClient, AnthropicClient and OllamaChatClient all implement the same surface (get_response(), get_streaming_response()). Agents, tools and workflows depend on the interface only.
🔀 Alternatives
① LiteLLM — external proxy speaking OpenAI's protocol; simple, but one extra hop & service.
② Hand-rolled adapter — exact fit for your domain, but you maintain streaming/tools/function-call differences.
③ Cloud gateways (Azure AI Gateway / AWS Bedrock) — great for compliance & quotas, still locks you to one cloud.
3 How to assemble conversations safely? expand
🧪 Example
Raw dict vs MAF's typed messages: 
# Raw dict — typos in "role" only surface at runtime
messages = [
  {"role": "sytsem", "content": "You are helpful"},  # typo!
  {"role": "user",   "content": "hi"},
]

# MAF — type-safe, IDE completes everything
from agent_framework import Message
messages = [
  Message("system", "You are helpful"),
  Message("user", "hi"),
]
❓ Why this matters
Real workloads carry more than strings: images, tool calls, tool results, thinking blocks, citations. Raw dicts force a project-wide if/else rewrite for each new kind; typed Content means adding a new content type leaves old code untouched.
✅ How MAF does it
See agent_framework/_types.py: Role is an enum (system / user / assistant / tool), Message holds contents: list[Content], where Content is a unified type built via factory methods like Content.from_text(). Wins: ① static checking + completion; ② multimodal & tool results fit naturally; ③ free serialization for Workflow cross-process transport.
🔀 Alternatives
① Raw dicts + JSON Schema validation — light, but still runtime-only;
② Roll your own Pydantic models — MAF already is Pydantic v2 under the hood, you're reinventing it;
③ LangChain's HumanMessage / AIMessage — same idea, weaker content-block coverage.
4 Why not hand-write tool-call JSON? expand
🧪 Example
Raw function-calling vs MAF's @tool: 
# Raw SDK: write schema, dispatch, feed result back yourself
tools = [{
  "type": "function",
  "function": {
    "name": "get_weather",
    "parameters": {"type": "object",
      "properties": {"city": {"type": "string"}},
      "required": ["city"]}}}]
# Then: parse tool_calls → find function → invoke → append result → call again…

# MAF: decorator generates schema, agent runs the loop
from agent_framework import tool

@tool
def get_weather(city: str) -> str:
    "Return the weather for a city."
    return f"{city} is 23°C"

agent = Agent(client=client, tools=[get_weather])
❓ Why this matters
Hand-written JSON schema breeds three bug families: ① parameter names/types drift from the function signature; ② you change the function and forget the schema; ③ each vendor supports a different JSON-Schema subset. Auto-generation makes the function the contract; refactor cost goes to zero.
✅ How MAF does it
@tool (see _tools.py) uses inspect + type hints to produce a JSON Schema, auto-injects it into ChatClient requests; when the model returns FunctionCallContent, the Agent — in the same loop — executes the function, appends FunctionResultContent to the messages, and calls the model again. No glue code from you.
🔀 Alternatives
① Hand-written schema — max control, max error rate;
② OpenAI's official function-calling SDK — OpenAI-only;
③ LangChain Tool / StructuredTool — similar idea, no native middleware/Workflow integration;
④ MCP protocol — out-of-process tool hosting; MAF can also surface MCP tools as local ones.
5 Multi-step & multi-agent without a while-monster? expand
🧪 Example
Hand-rolled loops go bad fast: 
messages = [...]
while True:
    resp = client.chat.completions.create(model=..., messages=messages, tools=tools)
    msg = resp.choices[0].message
    if not msg.tool_calls:
        break
    for tc in msg.tool_calls:
        result = dispatch(tc.function.name, json.loads(tc.function.arguments))
        messages.append({"role": "tool", ...})
    # No max iterations, no retries, no observability…

# MAF: delegated to agent.run() / Workflow
agent = Agent(client=client, tools=[get_weather, search_web])
result = await agent.run("Check Shanghai weather then suggest what to wear")
❓ Why this matters
"Loop" sounds simple. Productionizing it requires max-iteration limits, retries on errors, context trimming, parallel tools, middleware hooks (trace/cost), inter-agent message routing, resumable checkpoints… each item is hundreds of lines and many tests.
✅ How MAF does it
Single agent → built-in loop with DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS, middleware pipeline and streaming aggregation; multi-agent → agent_framework._workflows provides graph orchestration (nodes / edges / shared state / checkpoints / human approval), natively wired with ChatClient, tools and observability.
🔀 Alternatives
① LangGraph — original graph orchestrator, tightly coupled to LangChain;
② CrewAI — role-based multi-agent; ergonomic but light on productionization;
③ Hand-rolled state machines / Temporal / Durable Functions — robust but disconnected from LLM semantics, you rebuild the tool loop yourself.
✅ Key points
  • MAF = ChatClient (model) + Agent (loop) + Tools + Workflows + production features.
  • It does not train models; it owns the plumbing. Python and .NET both.
  • Positioning: the unified successor to Semantic Kernel + AutoGen, prototype to production.
💡 Design highlight
"Switch models with one line" is the default: the ChatClient is a vendor-agnostic abstraction, and agents / tools / workflows are all built on top — so portability is free from line one.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. Agent Framework makes 'switch model vendor' basically a one-line change. Which design mainly enables that?
  1. It copies every vendor's code into your project
  2. It trains the model for you
  3. A uniform ChatClient abstraction hides vendor differences behind one interface
  4. It supports only one vendor
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. An Agent depends on the abstract ChatClient, not a concrete vendor SDK; switching vendors just swaps a ChatClient implementation while agent/tool/memory logic stays put - the classic 'program to an interface' tradeoff.
2. Which is not one of the production headaches Agent Framework aims to smooth over?
  1. Observability (OpenTelemetry)
  2. Checkpointing and human-in-the-loop approval
  3. Choosing which ML algorithm to train your model with
  4. Durable / resumable workflows
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. Agent Framework owns the engineering plumbing around the model (orchestration, observability, durability, approval); it does not train the model itself - training is out of scope.
3. The minimal sample first builds a ChatClient, then wraps it in an Agent. Why not fold "talk to the model" and "agent behavior" into a single class?
  1. Because each vendor needs its own rewritten Agent
  2. Because Python forbids a class from doing two things
  3. Because merging them slows down model training
  4. ChatClient owns "how to talk to a vendor's model", Agent owns "persona / tools / loop"; splitting them means switching vendors changes only the ChatClient line while Agent logic stays put
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. ChatClient is the vendor-neutral connection layer; Agent is the behavior layer on top (name / instructions / tools / run loop). That separation is exactly why a vendor swap is "basically one line" - the same Agent wraps any ChatClient (Agent(client=…) or client.as_agent(…)).
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Suppose you already wrote a chat script directly against a vendor SDK. List the three pieces of boilerplate you expect to 'disappear' first after moving to Agent Framework, and say which framework concept (ChatClient / Message / @tool / Workflow) takes over each.
Part 1 · Big Picture

02 · Monorepo Tour

MAF is a monorepo: one repository holds both the Python and .NET implementations, plus docs, declarative agents and schemas. Learn the layout and you have a source-navigation map.

🏢 Analogy
Picture the repo as an office building: core is the foundation and load-bearing walls (everyone depends on it), each provider package is a department on a floor (OpenAI, Anthropic…), and lab is the rooftop R&D lab (cutting-edge, unstable). The Python tower and the .NET tower stand side by side with the same floor plan.

Top-level structure

PY/.NETpython/ · dotnet/
Two aligned implementations. This guide focuses on python/.
DOCSdocs/ · declarative-agents/ · schemas/
Architecture decision records (ADRs), declarative YAML agents, JSON schemas.

How the Python side is layered

COREpackages/core → agent_framework
Core abstractions: Agent / Message / tool / middleware / Workflows, plus built-in OpenAI / Azure OpenAI support.
PROVIDERSpackages/{foundry, anthropic, ollama, …}
Vendor / integration packages that extend core with concrete ChatClients & connectors.
LABpackages/lab
Experimental features: benchmarking, reinforcement learning, research.
Looking forGo to
Core types & Agent implpython/packages/core/agent_framework/
Public API (__all__)core/agent_framework/__init__.py
Beginner samplespython/samples/01-get-started/
.NET counterpartdotnet/src/Microsoft.Agents.AI*/
🔬 Detail · lazy loading
Provider submodules (e.g. agent_framework.azure) use __getattr__ for lazy loading: dependencies are pulled only on actual import, keeping core lightweight.
1 What lives inside the core package? expand
🧪 Example
core is "one file, one job": 
python/packages/core/agent_framework/
├── _agents.py        # Agent class, run() loop, DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS
├── _clients.py       # BaseChatClient ABC + Supports* protocols
├── _tools.py         # @tool decorator, FunctionTool class
├── _middleware.py    # middleware pipeline: trace / retry / custom
├── _types.py         # Message / Role / Content union
├── _workflows/       # graph orchestration: nodes, edges, state, checkpoints
├── observability/    # OpenTelemetry traces / cost / token metrics
└── __init__.py       # public API — what 'from agent_framework import' exposes
❓ Why this matters
If core is split into dozens of micro-packages, newcomers spend their first week learning imports; if it's a single god-module, you get circular deps, slow loads and untestable seams. "Single package, many internal files" is the standard, stable compromise: one import path, clean inner layering.
✅ How MAF does it
All abstractions are re-exported from agent_framework (Agent, Message, tool, WorkflowBuilder…); files prefixed with _ are internal and surfaced via __all__ in __init__.py — internal refactors never break user code.
🔀 Alternatives
① Single-file god-module — fastest start, long-term disaster;
② Micro-package architecture (one PyPI package per abstraction) — dependency hell;
③ Plugin-only — core ships interfaces, everything else is a plugin; flexible, no batteries included.
2 How does provider lazy-loading actually work? expand
🧪 Example
Submodule __init__.py defers imports with __getattr__: 
# agent_framework/openai/__init__.py (sketch)
import importlib
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from ._chat_client import OpenAIChatClient

_LAZY = {"OpenAIChatClient": "._chat_client"}

def __getattr__(name: str):
    if name in _LAZY:
        mod = importlib.import_module(_LAZY[name], __name__)
        return getattr(mod, name)
    raise AttributeError(name)

__all__ = list(_LAZY)
So import agent_framework doesn't drag in the openai SDK; it loads only when you actually touch OpenAIChatClient.
❓ Why this matters
① Startup speed — each extra SDK adds tens-to-hundreds of ms; critical for CLI & serverless;
② Optional dependencies — a user who only installed the OpenAI extras shouldn't crash on Anthropic imports;
③ Install size — core stays minimal, extras opt-in via pip install agent-framework[azure].
✅ How MAF does it
PEP 562 module-level __getattr__ combined with a TYPE_CHECKING-guarded real import — keeps lazy semantics at runtime while preserving full IDE / mypy navigation. Pyright and PyCharm jump-to-definition still work.
🔀 Alternatives
① Eager imports — simple but slow + polluted deps;
② entry_points / plugin protocol — full decoupling, harder discovery & debugging;
③ try/except ImportError — common trick, doesn't scale to many providers.
3 How are Python and .NET kept "aligned"? expand
🧪 Example
Same concept, two syntaxes: 
# Python
from agent_framework import Agent
from agent_framework.openai import OpenAIChatClient
agent = Agent(
    client=OpenAIChatClient(model="gpt-4o"),
    name="weather-bot",
    instructions="You are a weather assistant",
)
await agent.run("Shanghai weather tomorrow?")
// C#
using Microsoft.Agents.AI;
using Microsoft.Agents.AI.OpenAI;

var agent = new ChatClientAgent(
    chatClient: new OpenAIChatClient("gpt-4o"),
    name: "weather-bot",
    instructions: "You are a weather assistant");
var result = await agent.RunAsync("Shanghai weather?");
❓ Why this matters
In real enterprises, ML/data teams prefer Python while business backends are often .NET. If concepts diverge, communication becomes translation, docs are written twice and migrations are painful. Alignment means one set of docs powers both sides.
✅ How MAF does it
Both implementations align on concepts, naming and lifecycle: same Agent / ChatClient / Tool / Workflow, same run / stream / observability hooks; only the syntax follows local idioms (async/await, decorators vs attributes, type hints vs strong static types).
🔀 Alternatives
① Single language only — miss half the market;
② Auto-generate from IDL / proto — easy to over-abstract, fights idioms;
③ FFI / wrappers (Python calls .NET DLLs or vice versa) — deployment & debugging nightmares.
4 .NET naming correspondence expand
🧪 Example
Main symbol map:
Python.NETMeaning
agent_frameworkMicrosoft.Agents.AIcore package
AgentChatClientAgentagent entity (with loop)
ChatClientIChatClientvendor-agnostic client
Message / RoleMessage / ChatRoletyped messages
@tool[Description] + AIFunctionFactoryturn function into tool
_workflows/Microsoft.Agents.AI.Workflowsgraph orchestration
observability/Microsoft.Extensions.AI.TelemetryOTel integration
agent.run() / .run_stream()RunAsync() / RunStreamingAsync()sync & streaming entry
❓ Why this matters
Naming alignment = mental-model alignment: what you learn in Python ("ChatClient is the vendor abstraction") transfers directly to your .NET teammates. Doc search, code review and migration all benefit.
✅ How MAF does it
The naming table lives in ADRs under docs/decisions; new features must land in both implementations or they don't ship — making "reading the Python tutorial also teaches you .NET" a real outcome.
🔀 Alternatives
① Pure idiomatic naming on both sides — readers translate forever;
② IDL-generated bindings — uniform names, often unidiomatic;
③ Shared IDL docs, independent code — pragmatic middle ground, what MAF is gradually moving toward.
✅ Key points
  • One repo, two aligned implementations: python/ and dotnet/.
  • Python: core is the foundation, provider packages extend on demand, lab holds the frontier.
  • To read the source, start at core/agent_framework/__init__.py's __all__.
💡 Design highlight
core depends on no specific vendor: vendor code lives in separate provider packages and is wired in via lazy loading. The core API stays stable; adding a vendor never touches the foundation.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. core puts Agent / Message / tool / Workflows all in one agent_framework package (one package, many _-prefixed files) instead of dozens of tiny PyPI packages. The main benefit?
  1. One public import path, internal refactors don't touch user code, while avoiding the dependency hell of a micro-package architecture
  2. It makes the program run faster
  3. It makes core depend on nothing
  4. It forces everyone onto .NET
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. "One package, many files" is the classic big-framework compromise: outside it's a single from agent_framework import …; inside, _-prefixed files layer the code and __init__.py's __all__ exposes the public API. Micro-packages bring version/dependency nightmares; a god-module brings circular imports and slow loads - one-package-many-files balances both.
2. import agent_framework does not pull in heavy deps like azure-identity or anthropic; they load only when you actually access the matching ChatClient. How?
  1. Provider submodules use module-level __getattr__ (PEP 562) for lazy loading - importing only on access
  2. Every import eagerly loads every vendor
  3. All provider deps are bundled into core
  4. A global try/except swallows missing deps
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. A submodule's __init__.py defers imports via __getattr__: faster startup (each un-loaded SDK saves tens of ms), optional deps don't error at import time when absent, and install size stays on-demand (pip install agent-framework[azure]). core stays lightweight and adding a vendor never touches core.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Suppose you write a provider package for a new vendor. Following the monorepo's conventions, where should it live, what does it depend on, and does it require changes to core? Explain why the "adding a vendor never touches core" rule is worth keeping.
Part 1 · Big Picture

03 · Lifecycle of a Run

You write just one line — await agent.run("…") — but a chain of events happens underneath. Understand this lifecycle and every later "internals" lesson gets easy.

🍽️ Analogy
An Agent is like a waiter: you order (input) → they combine your words with the "house rules" (instructions) into a standard ticket (messages) → hand it to the kitchen (ChatClient → LLM) → if the chef says "fetch an ingredient first" (tool call), the waiter fetches it (executes the tool) and brings the result back → until the dish is ready (final answer), served to you (AgentResponse).

At a glance: the run pipeline

Your code
run("…")
→
Agent assembles
instructions + history + tools
→
ChatClient
vendor-agnostic
→
LLM
reply / function_call
→
Tool loop
execute→feed back→rethink
→
AgentResponse
final answer

The full data flow of one run

1

Your input

A string or a list of Message, arriving together with conversation history.

2

Agent assembles

Combine instructions (system prompt) + history + new input + available tools into one request.

3

ChatClient call

The vendor-agnostic interface sends it to a concrete LLM (Foundry / OpenAI / …) and returns a ChatResponse.

4

Need a tool?

If the response contains a function_call, the Agent executes the function, appends the function_result to the messages, and loops back to step 3.

5

Converge & return

When the model stops asking for tools, the loop ends with an AgentResponse (or a stream of AgentResponseUpdate when streaming).

Lifecycle stages at a glance

StageWho does itOutput
InputYour codestring / Message (+ optional session)
AssembleAgent (_agents.py)instructions + history + input + tools schema
CallChatClientChatResponse (may carry function_call)
Tool loopAgent + your functionFunctionResultContent fed back, loops to "Call"
ConvergeAgentAgentResponse / stream of AgentResponseUpdate
🌍 Big picture
This is the classic agent loop (think → act → observe): the model "thinks", the framework "acts" for it (runs tools), feeds the "observation" back, until done. You call run() once; the loop runs to completion internally.
1 In multi-turn chat, where does history come from? expand
🧪 Example
Pass the same session across runs and history accumulates automatically: 
from agent_framework import Agent
agent = Agent(client=client, instructions="…")
session = agent.create_session()
await agent.run("My name is Sam", session=session)
await agent.run("What's my name?", session=session)  # remembers "Sam"
(Sessions & memory are Lesson 07; here just note history rides on the "session".)
2 What does message assembly actually look like? expand
🧪 Example
The list sent to the LLM on one run() roughly looks like: 
[
  SystemMessage("You are a concise weather assistant."),  # ← instructions
  UserMessage("My name is Sam"),                          # ← history 1
  AssistantMessage("Hi Sam!"),                             # ← history 2
  UserMessage("Weather in Shanghai today?"),               # ← this input
]
tools = [
  {"name": "get_weather", "parameters": {...}},   # ← auto-generated
]
Instructions go first, history in chronological order, current input last, tool schemas attached alongside.
❓ Why this matters
Three hidden traps: ① system position — some models require system at index 0; ② context-window budget — history + tool schema + input must fit in the token cap; ③ tool-schema overhead — 20 tools can eat several thousand tokens.
✅ How MAF does it
In _agents.py the Agent assembles in a fixed order: instructions → system, tools → schema, history → ordered messages, with fallbacks for content kinds a ChatClient doesn't support. Callers only think about business input; assembly is the framework's job.
🔀 Alternatives
① Fully manual assembly — maximum flexibility, maximum footguns;
② Templated (Jinja / Handlebars) — great for prompt-heavy workflows, loses type safety;
③ Pure middleware pipeline — every step as middleware; flexible but long debug chain.
3 Where does the tool loop stop? expand
🧪 Example
The Agent ships with an iteration cap that breaks the loop: 
# agent_framework/_agents.py
DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS = 10

class Agent:
    def __init__(self, ..., max_iterations: int = DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS):
        self.max_iterations = max_iterations

    async def run(self, input, ...):
        for i in range(self.max_iterations):
            resp = await self._client.get_response(...)
            if not _has_tool_calls(resp):
                return AgentResponse(...)
            await self._execute_tools(resp)
        # Cap hit: return a partial result with a reason
        return AgentResponse(..., terminated_reason="max_iterations")
Tune per agent: Agent(..., max_iterations=20).
❓ Why this matters
Uncapped loops = bill explosion: a model can re-call the same tool forever, deadlock between dependent tools, or be steered by prompt injection. Even normal usage needs a stop-loss.
✅ How MAF does it
Default DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS ships a sane value, overridable per Agent; when it trips, the call returns a structured terminated_reason instead of raising — upper layers can choose to retry, escalate or surface an error.
🔀 Alternatives
① No limit — outright dangerous;
② Token-budget based — more economical, harder to implement;
③ Wall-clock timeout — protects latency-sensitive paths;
④ Human approval per iteration — most expensive, safest (finance/medical); also doable via Workflow human approval.
4 Streaming vs non-streaming internals expand
🧪 Example
Same Agent, two entry points: 
# Non-streaming: one full result
result: AgentResponse = await agent.run("Write a short autumn poem")
print(result.text)

# Streaming: AgentResponseUpdate chunks
async for update in agent.run_stream("Write a short autumn poem"):
    if update.text:
        print(update.text, end="", flush=True)
❓ Why this matters
① Time-to-first-token matters most for long answers;
② UX — non-streamed long answers feel like a 30-second freeze; streaming feels like a typewriter;
③ Early debugging / cancellation — streaming lets you spot drift early and abort.
✅ How MAF does it
Same Agent, same tools: run() returns a complete AgentResponse; run_stream() returns AsyncIterable[AgentResponseUpdate], emitting text, tool calls and thinking blocks as chunks — all over the same internal loop, so switching modes doesn't change your business code.
🔀 Alternatives
① Pure SSE push — simple protocol, you handle reconnect & heartbeats;
② WebSocket — bidirectional, better for live interaction;
③ Polling — broadest compatibility, worst UX;
④ Client-side buffering — reassemble chunks into final text on the client; MAF supports this too.
5 Error handling inside the loop expand
🧪 Example
Three classic failures: 
# 1) LLM-side error: 429 / 500 / timeout
   → ChatClient raises ChatClientError

# 2) Tool function raises
@tool
def get_weather(city: str) -> str:
    raise RuntimeError("weather API down")
   → Agent wraps it as FunctionResultContent(error=...),
     hands it back to the model to degrade or apologize

# 3) Tool execution timeout
   → middleware layer catches, writes a trace span
❓ Why this matters
Inside an agent loop, "retry / skip / abort / partial" can all be the right call — there's no silver bullet. The framework has to provide a structured, customizable failure model, or you'll either swallow errors silently or crash constantly.
✅ How MAF does it
Errors flow through the middleware pipeline (_middleware.py): users register retry policies, circuit breakers, fallbacks; tool exceptions become FunctionResultContent fed back to the model; unrecoverable failures surface on AgentResponse with a terminated_reason and original exception chain for structured upstream handling.
🔀 Alternatives
① try/except everywhere — fast to write, near-zero observability;
② Circuit breaker — protects downstream, watch for false trips;
③ Let-it-crash (Erlang style) — restart the agent on failure, rely on upstream retries — simple but costly.
✅ Key points
  • One run() = assemble messages → call LLM → (loop tools as needed) → return AgentResponse.
  • Tool calling is a loop, not one-shot: the model may request tools multiple times.
  • The streaming variant yields AgentResponseUpdate chunks that compose the full response.
💡 Design highlight
The loop is encapsulated in the framework: callers see only "input → output", while the think–act–think complexity is absorbed by the Agent — that's why it's calmer than raw LLM SDK calls.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. You wrote just one line await agent.run("…"), yet when the model needs tools it loops "call model → run tool → call model" several rounds. Where does that loop run?
  1. Inside run(), completed automatically - you call it just once
  2. You must write your own while loop calling run() repeatedly
  3. Inside each tool function's own code
  4. While print() renders output
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. This is the classic agent loop (think → act → observe): the model emits a function_call, the framework runs the tool, appends the function_result to the messages, and returns to "call model" until the model stops asking for tools (capped at DEFAULT_MAX_ITERATIONS=40). You only run once.
2. In a multi-turn chat, what fundamentally makes "the model remember earlier turns" work?
  1. The model keeps a block of persistent memory inside it
  2. The finish_reason field carries the history
  3. The framework keeps everyone's chat in one global variable
  4. Passing the same session (AgentSession) into each run(), so history accumulates into the message list
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. run() is stateless by default; for continuity you call agent.create_session() then run(…, session=session) each time, and user messages + replies append to the session's history. The only carrier of state is the growing message list, not anything the model "remembers".
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • When run() assembles a request it stitches instructions / history / current input / tool schemas together for the model. If your Agent has 20 tools and a long conversation, which part do you expect to fill the context window first? Use that to discuss the token cost and tradeoffs of "number of tools" vs "history length".
Part 2 · User's View

04 · Messages & Content

Talking to a model is really passing a list of messages back and forth. MAF makes messages structured objects (Message) instead of bare dicts — type-safe, and able to carry tool calls, images and more.

✉️ Analogy
A Message is like an envelope: the outside states the sender's identity (role: user / assistant / system / tool), and inside you can place several content cards (contents: some text, an image, a tool call, a tool result…).

Three core types

TypeRoleFrom
Messageone message = role + contents_types.py
Roleidentity: "system"/"user"/"assistant"/"tool"_types.py
Contentunified content type, built via factory methods_types.py

Hand-built dicts vs structured messages

😣 Bare dicts

messages = [
  {"role": "system", "content": "You are a bot"},
  {"role": "user", "content": "hi"},
]
# typo-prone keys; tool calls by hand

🙂 Message objects

from agent_framework import Message
messages = [
  Message("system", "You are a bot"),
  Message("user", "hi"),
]
# type-safe, carries any Content

Message's second argument contents accepts a string (auto-converted to text), a Content object, or a list of them. Read the text via msg.text.

1 What can a Content hold? click to expand
🧪 Example
Content is a unified type; class methods create each kind: 
Content.from_text("some text")
Content.from_data(data=..., media_type="image/png")  # image/binary
Content.from_uri(uri="https://…", media_type="image/png")
Content.from_function_call(...)      # model wants a tool
Content.from_function_result(...)    # tool execution result
So one Message can mix text and images, and carry tool calls / results.
❓ Why this matters
LLM capabilities go beyond "pure text Q&A": GPT-4o and Claude support image inputs, models can request tool calls and receive results. If each content kind uses separate fields (text, image_url, tool_calls…), the message shape becomes complex and non-extensible. Unifying into a Content list means code only cares about "a message has multiple content blocks", with no special-casing per type.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF uses a discriminated union: every Content has a type field ("text"/"data"/"uri"/"function_call"/"function_result"); the framework routes by type. Adding a new content kind (video, audio) only requires a new type; old messages still parse. Pydantic ensures type safety, IDEs provide full hints.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI SDK: text in content field, images in content array, tool calls in tool_calls field — three inconsistent shapes requiring branching logic.
  • Anthropic: uses content array + type discrimination similar to MAF, but each vendor differs.
  • MAF unifies them all into one format, zero code changes across vendors.
2 Constructing Messages click to expand
🧪 Example
# Plain text (string auto-wrapped)
msg = Message("user", "hello")

# Single Content object
msg = Message("user", Content.from_text("hello"))

# Multiple Content: mixed text + image
msg = Message("user", [
    Content.from_text("What's in this image?"),
    Content.from_uri(uri="https://example.com/img.png", media_type="image/png")
])

# Text shortcut
text = msg.text  # auto-extracts text from contents
❓ Why this matters
Multimodal conversations are increasingly common: a user sends an image + a question; the model replies with text + a chart; tool calls and results must coexist with text in the same message. If Message only holds a string, you'd need multiple messages or complex manual structures. Allowing a heterogeneous content list lets one message naturally express "text + image + tool call".
✅ How MAF does it
Message's contents parameter accepts:
  • String: auto-converts to Content.from_text(...)
  • Single Content: wrapped into a list
  • Content list: stored directly
Also provides .text property that auto-concatenates all text contents (joins multiple text blocks with spaces). Users can use simple or complex forms as needed; mental model stays unified.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI: text messages vs multi-content messages use different formats ({role, content: "str"} vs {role, content: [{type, ...}]}), tool calls add a tool_calls array. Framework must detect format before parsing.
  • LangChain: HumanMessage/AIMessage have different constructors.
  • MAF unified constructor: type-safe + IDE hints + multimodal out-of-the-box.
3 Tool calls as message flow click to expand
🧪 Example
# Model output: assistant message with function_call
msg_call = Message("assistant", [
    Content.from_function_call(
        name="get_weather",
        arguments={"location": "Seattle"},
        call_id="call_abc123"
    )
])

# Framework feedback: role="tool" message with function_result
msg_result = Message("tool", [
    Content.from_function_result(
        name="get_weather",
        result="Sunny, 72°F",
        call_id="call_abc123"
    )
])
# These two append to history; model continues with final answer
❓ Why this matters
Tool calls are multi-turn interactions:
  1. Model decides "need tool", outputs function_call
  2. Framework executes tool, gets result
  3. Framework appends result to conversation; model reads and produces final answer
If tool calls/results aren't standard messages, you need a separate pipeline. Embedding them in Message makes the entire conversation a message chain, no special logic required.
✅ How MAF does it
Content.from_function_call and .from_function_result are two special content types fully integrated into the message system:
  • Type unification: tool call = assistant message + function_call content; tool result = tool message + function_result content.
  • Consistent serialization: history replay, logs, debugging all use the same JSON structure for tool calls and regular messages.
  • Extensible: future "parallel tool calls" just needs multiple function_call contents in one message.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI: assistant message adds tool_calls array; tool message uses role="tool" + tool_call_id. Two field sets.
  • Anthropic: content array holds tool_use/tool_result blocks, similar to MAF but each vendor differs.
  • MAF abstracts the differences: developers use one API, framework handles vendor conversion.
4 Serialization click to expand
🧪 Example
# Message → dict (JSON-serializable)
msg = Message("user", "Hello")
d = msg.to_dict()
# → {"role": "user", "contents": [{"type": "text", "text": "Hello"}]}

# dict → Message (restore from JSON)
msg_restored = Message.from_dict(d)
assert msg_restored.role == "user"
assert msg_restored.text == "Hello"

# Tool calls also fully serialize
msg_with_tool = Message("assistant", [
    Content.from_function_call(name="foo", arguments={"x": 1})
])
d2 = msg_with_tool.to_dict()
msg_back = Message.from_dict(d2)  # fully restored
❓ Why this matters
Real applications need:
  • Persistence: store conversation history in databases (Redis, Cosmos, PostgreSQL).
  • Replay debugging: capture production message flows, reproduce locally.
  • Audit logs: compliance requires saving all conversations (including tool calls).
  • Cross-service passing: microservices pass message context.
Without lossless serialization, tool calls, images and other complex content lose information.
✅ How MAF does it
Message.to_dict() / from_dict(...) are fully reversible:
  • All Content types (text/data/uri/function_call/function_result) serialize losslessly.
  • Returned dict is pure JSON, can be directly stored, transmitted, logged.
  • Pydantic models auto-handle type validation; deserialization raises on type errors.
  • Third-party persistence libs (like redis, azure-cosmos) can directly store dicts.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI SDK: native messages are already dicts, but format may change per version, no type guarantees.
  • LangChain: Message.dict() returns nested structures, field names unstable, requires manual handling.
  • Manual JSON: fully custom, but schema evolution easily breaks compatibility.
  • MAF versioned schema: framework upgrades auto-migrate old data (see compatibility layer in _types.py).
5 Comparison with OpenAI format click to expand
🧪 Example
ScenarioOpenAI native formatMAF format
Plain text
{"role": "user",
 "content": "hi"}
Message("user", "hi")
Tool call
{"role": "assistant",
 "tool_calls": [{
   "type": "function",
   "function": {"name": "get_weather", ...}
 }]}
Message("assistant", [
  Content.from_function_call(
    name="get_weather", ...
  )
])
Tool result
{"role": "tool",
 "content": "result",
 "tool_call_id": "x"}
Message("tool", [
  Content.from_function_result(
    result="result", call_id="x"
  )
])
❓ Why this matters
Problems with using OpenAI dicts directly:
  • Vendor lock-in: switching to Anthropic/Ollama/Azure requires extensive changes.
  • Scattered fields: content / tool_calls / tool_call_id at different nesting levels, complex parsing.
  • No type safety: typos only caught at runtime.
  • Inconsistent format: plain text vs image vs tool call use different structures.
An abstraction layer lets you write once, run anywhere.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF provides unified abstraction with bidirectional conversion:
  • BaseChatClient subclasses (like OpenAIChatClient) auto-convert Message to vendor format, call API, convert back.
  • Conversion is encapsulated inside the client (private _prepare_messages_for_openai, see python/packages/openai/agent_framework_openai/_chat_client.py); you rarely call it directly.
  • Type safety: IDE auto-completes Message(...) / Content.from_..., catches errors at edit time.
  • Testable: unit tests don't depend on real LLMs, just mock Message.
🔀 Alternatives
  • Use SDK dicts directly: zero abstraction, but vendor switch requires complete rewrite.
  • LangChain MessageType: provides abstraction, but HumanMessage/AIMessage/ToolMessage classification too granular, increases mental load.
  • LlamaIndex ChatMessage: simple and unified, but limited functionality, weak multimodal/tool support.
  • MAF just right: one Message + five Content types covers all scenarios, seamless vendor switching.
✅ Key points
  • A conversation = a list of Message; each = role + contents.
  • Role has four values: system / user / assistant / tool.
  • Content is one unified type built via from_text/from_data/from_function_call…
💡 Design highlight
Text, images, tool calls and tool results are unified into one Content, discriminated by a type field. The message shape stays simple yet extensible — adding a content kind breaks nothing.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. Instead of a separate class per content kind (text / image / function call / result), MAF uses one unified Content with a type field. The core benefit?
  1. A Message's contents is a list of Content, each carrying a type ("text"/"uri"/"function_call"…); the framework routes by type, and a new kind is just a new type while old messages keep working
  2. Because it makes the model reason more accurately
  3. Because each vendor needs its own Message class
  4. Because Python can't define multiple classes
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. This is a discriminated union: with a unified Content list, code only cares that "a message has several content blocks"; mixed text+image and function call/result all share one container, built via factory methods Content.from_text() / from_uri() / from_function_call(). It normalizes every vendor's format into one, so business code never changes.
2. What type is a message's role ("system"/"user"/"assistant"/"tool") in MAF?
  1. A subclass per role
  2. A closed Enum that can't be extended
  3. An integer id
  4. Role is string-based (a NewType over str) - use the string value directly, even custom roles
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. Role = NewType("Role", str), so you just write Message("user", […]). Choosing a string over a closed Enum is an extensibility tradeoff: future or vendor-specific roles need no framework change; the cost is losing the Enum's compile-time guardrails, backed instead by convention and docs.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Why is Message.contents a list rather than a single content? Give a real scenario that needs multiple blocks (hint: multimodal, or text + a function call in one turn), and explain how that helps "one format across vendors".
Part 2 · User's View

05 · ChatClient & Creating Agents

A ChatClient is the vendor-agnostic "connect to a model" interface; an Agent adds a name, system instructions, tools and a loop on top. This lesson builds an Agent from a ChatClient and runs it with run.

🎧 Analogy
A ChatClient is like a walkie-talkie: it can talk to a channel (model) but has no persona. An Agent is the operator holding that walkie-talkie who has been briefed on duties (instructions) and tools — you talk to the operator, who gets the job done.

Two ways to create

A: Agent(client=…)

from agent_framework import Agent
agent = Agent(
  client=client,
  name="HelloAgent",
  instructions="You are friendly.",
)

B: client.as_agent(…)

agent = client.as_agent(
  name="HelloAgent",
  instructions="You are friendly.",
)
# equivalent, reads nicer

Both work; as_agent reads more fluently. Same Agent underneath.

From ChatClient to Agent

ChatClient
talks to a model
→
.as_agent(…)
add name / instructions / tools
→
Agent
agent with the loop
→
agent.run(…)
run it

Run it: blocking and streaming

non-streaming vs streaming
# non-streaming: full answer at once
result = await agent.run("What is the capital of France?")
print(result)

# streaming: tokens as they arrive
async for chunk in agent.run("Tell me a fun fact.", stream=True):
    if chunk.text:
        print(chunk.text, end="", flush=True)

One run(): without stream it returns a full AgentResponse; with stream=True it returns an async iterator yielding AgentResponseUpdate chunks.

run() vs run(stream=True)

Aspectrun("…") (non-streaming)run("…", stream=True) (streaming)
Return typeAgentResponseasync iterator of AgentResponseUpdate
How to consumeresult = await agent.run(…)async for chunk in agent.run(…, stream=True)
Time-to-first-tokenwait for full generationchunks arrive immediately
When to usebatch / scripts / need full resultchat UI / long answers / live display
🔬 Switching vendors
Replace FoundryChatClient with OpenAIChatClient, AnthropicClient, OllamaChatClient… and the Agent layer doesn't change at all. Each client comes from its provider package (Lesson 16).
1 as_agent vs Agent(client=...) click to expand
🧪 Example
# Approach A: direct construction
agent_a = Agent(
    client=client,
    name="MyAgent",
    instructions="You are helpful."
)

# Approach B: syntactic sugar
agent_b = client.as_agent(
    name="MyAgent",
    instructions="You are helpful."
)

# Completely equivalent
assert type(agent_a) == type(agent_b)  # both Agent
❓ Why this matters
In real scenarios, you might first create a ChatClient (with model config, API keys, retry policies), then derive multiple Agents (different personas, different tools). Both patterns must be supported:
  • Agent(client=...): fits "Agent is the protagonist" scenarios, explicit dependency injection.
  • client.as_agent(...): fits "client is the protagonist" scenarios, method chaining more natural.
A single pattern makes some code styles awkward.
✅ How MAF does it
as_agent is syntactic sugar, internally calling Agent(client=self, ...) (see BaseChatClient.as_agent in python/packages/core/agent_framework/_clients.py). Both patterns return the same type, behavior identical. Users can choose based on context:
  • Testing with mocks: Agent(client=mock_client, ...)
  • Configuring multiple Agents: agents = [client.as_agent(...) for cfg in configs]
Framework doesn't force style, but guarantees semantic unity.
🔀 Alternatives
  • LangChain: no as_agent pattern, must manually wrap ChatOpenAI in AgentExecutor.
  • Semantic Kernel: uses Kernel as container, Agent concept weaker.
  • OpenAI Assistants API: Agents are server-side, no local construction.
  • MAF supports both: sugar lowers onboarding barrier, direct construction preserves flexibility.
2 run() parameters click to expand
🧪 Example
# Minimal: message only
result = await agent.run("hello")

# With session: multi-turn
session = agent.create_session()
result = await agent.run("I'm Alice", session=session)
result = await agent.run("What's my name?", session=session)

# Streaming: chunk-by-chunk
async for chunk in agent.run("Tell a story", stream=True):
    print(chunk.text, end="")

# Combined: session + streaming
async for chunk in agent.run("Continue", session=session, stream=True):
    print(chunk.text, end="")
❓ Why this matters
Agent use cases vary:
  • One-shot query: stateless, just need an answer (FAQ bot).
  • Multi-turn conversation: remember context (customer service, assistant).
  • Streaming response: show generation in real-time (UX need).
  • Combined: multi-turn + streaming (chat app).
If each scenario uses different methods (run, run_with_session, run_stream…), the API bloats and learning curve steepens. Unifying into one run() with optional parameters is cleaner.
✅ How MAF does it
run() signature:
async def run(
    self,
    message: str | Message,
    session: AgentSession | None = None,
    stream: bool = False,
    ...
) -> AgentResponse | AsyncIterator[AgentResponseUpdate]:
  • Required param only message, everything else optional.
  • session defaults None: omit for stateless, pass for multi-turn.
  • stream defaults False: blocking full result; True returns async iterator.
  • Return type determined by stream: type checkers (mypy, Pylance) infer correctly.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI SDK: create(...) vs create(..., stream=True), but Assistants API has separate runs.create.
  • LangChain: invoke, ainvoke, stream, astream — four methods.
  • Anthropic SDK: messages.create vs messages.stream.
  • MAF one method, parameters control behavior, minimal mental load.
3 Streaming internals click to expand
🧪 Example
async for chunk in agent.run("Write a poem", stream=True):
    print(f"type={chunk.type}")      # "text_delta" / "tool_call" / "final"
    print(f"text={chunk.text}")      # text delta for this chunk
    print(f"is_final={chunk.is_final}")  # whether last chunk
    
    if chunk.is_final:
        print(f"full_response={chunk.response}")  # complete AgentResponse
❓ Why this matters
Streaming responses aren't just "typewriter effect":
  • Lower perceived latency: time-to-first-token (TTFT) matters more than total duration.
  • Long outputs: generating hundreds or thousands of tokens, users can't wait for completion.
  • Early cancellation: user sees wrong direction, stops immediately (saves cost).
  • Real-time processing: parse while generating (e.g., live translation, code highlighting).
Just returning raw text stream isn't enough: need to know "tool call started", "final completion" events to update UI correctly.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF streams AgentResponseUpdate objects (see python/packages/core/agent_framework/_types.py):
  • type field: "text_delta" (text increment) / "tool_call" (tool invocation) / "final" (end).
  • text field: new text in this chunk.
  • is_final: True for last chunk; .response property holds complete AgentResponse.
  • Metadata: usage (token consumption), finish_reason (why stopped), etc.
Framework auto-accumulates text, parses tool calls, handles multi-turn loops; user just async for consumes.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI SDK: returns SSE event stream, user must manually parse delta, accumulate text, detect end.
  • Anthropic SDK: MessageStream object, many event types (content_block_start, content_block_delta…), steep learning curve.
  • LangChain: astream returns raw chunks, format depends on underlying LLM.
  • MAF unified Update object, abstracts vendor differences, high-level semantics ("text delta", "tool call", "done") clear.
4 Sharing a client across Agents click to expand
🧪 Example
client = FoundryChatClient(model="gpt-4o", api_key=...)

# Create multiple Agents, different personas
weather_agent = client.as_agent(
    name="WeatherBot",
    instructions="You are a weather assistant.",
    tools=[get_weather]
)

translator_agent = client.as_agent(
    name="Translator",
    instructions="Translate to French."
)

support_agent = client.as_agent(
    name="Support",
    instructions="Answer user questions.",
    tools=[search_kb, create_ticket]
)

# All three Agents reuse same HTTP connection pool, API key
❓ Why this matters
Common production scenarios:
  • Multi-role systems: one app has multiple Agents (router, executor, reviewer), all calling the same model.
  • Connection pooling: HTTP/2 supports multiplexing, one TCP connection handles concurrent requests. Each Agent creating its own connection wastes resources.
  • Config reuse: API key, retry policies, timeouts configured once.
  • Monitoring / rate-limiting: all requests through one client, unified instrumentation and throttling.
If Agent and ChatClient are coupled (each Agent internally creates a client), connection sharing is impossible.
✅ How MAF does it
ChatClient is stateless (see BaseChatClient):
  • Doesn't hold conversation history (history lives in AgentSession).
  • Only responsible for "send request → parse response", can be called concurrently by multiple Agents.
  • Internally uses httpx.AsyncClient (Python) or HttpClient (.NET), auto-manages connection pools.
  • Thread-safe (or async-safe): multiple await agent.run(...) can execute concurrently.
This design maximizes resource utilization and follows dependency injection principles (Agent depends on ChatClient abstraction).
🔀 Alternatives
  • Each Agent creates internal client: simple but wasteful. OpenAI Assistants API uses server-side Agents, no such issue.
  • Global singleton client: hard to test, hard to configure different environments.
  • LangChain: ChatOpenAI can be shared, but docs don't emphasize it; users often create new instances each time.
  • MAF explicit design: client.as_agent(...) syntax hints "multiple agents share one client".
✅ Key points
  • ChatClient = the model (vendor-agnostic); Agent = ChatClient + persona + tools + loop.
  • Create with Agent(client=…) or client.as_agent(…).
  • run() returns the full response; run(stream=True) streams chunks.
💡 Design highlight
Streaming and non-streaming share one run(), toggled by a single stream flag. One mental model, no second API.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. What's the relationship between Agent(client=…) and client.as_agent(…)?
  1. They produce incompatible classes
  2. They're equivalent - both build the same Agent; as_agent is just a convenience factory on the ChatClient
  3. Agent(client=) is sync, as_agent is async
  4. as_agent trains a new model
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. as_agent is a convenience factory on the ChatClient that internally does Agent(client=self, …). Two spellings are just style; either way, switching vendors changes only the client construction line - the Agent's behavior layer (name / instructions / tools) stays put.
2. With the same agent.run(), how do you switch from "full answer at once" to "token-by-token streaming"?
  1. Pass stream=True: returns an async iterator yielding AgentResponseUpdate chunks; omit it for a complete AgentResponse
  2. Call a different method run_stream_v2()
  3. Write "please stream" in the instructions
  4. Build a separate StreamingAgent class
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. Streaming and non-streaming share one run(); the stream flag switches the return shape: a full AgentResponse vs a sequence of AgentResponseUpdate. Upper-layer code stays structurally similar - just render chunk.text incrementally when streaming.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • How does streaming (stream=True) affect "perceived latency" vs "code complexity"? In which scenarios would you actually prefer non-streaming (one AgentResponse)?
Part 2 · User's View

06 · Tools

Tools let an Agent not just "talk" but "do": fetch weather, read a database, call an API. Write an ordinary Python function, tag it with @tool, and the model can call it.

🧰 Analogy
The model is a smart brain with short arms: it knows "time to check the weather" but can't reach the thermometer. Tools are the handy instruments you hand it, each with a manual (function signature + docstring) it follows.

Define a tool

samples/01-get-started/02_add_tools.py
from typing import Annotated
from agent_framework import Agent, tool
from pydantic import Field

@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def get_weather(
    location: Annotated[str, Field(description="The location to get weather for.")],
) -> str:
    """Get the weather for a given location."""
    return f"It's sunny in {location}."

agent = Agent(client=client, name="WeatherAgent",
    instructions="Use get_weather to answer.",
    tools=[get_weather])

result = await agent.run("What's the weather in Seattle?")

The tool-call pipeline

@tool function
plain Python function
→
JSON Schema
auto-built from signature
→
Model picks
emits a function_call
→
Framework runs
calls your function
→
Result fed back
function_result to the model

Three things happen automatically

1

Generate schema

A JSON Schema is built from the signature + Annotated[..., Field(description=…)] + docstring, and handed to the model.

2

Model decides to call

The model emits a function_call (function name + arguments) in its response.

3

Framework executes & feeds back

The Agent runs your function, appends the function_result, and lets the model continue.

Function parts → schema fields

This part of the functionMaps to schemaExample
function namenameget_weather → "get_weather"
docstringdescription"Get the weather…" → what it does
param type hintparameters.typestr → "string", int → "integer"
Field(description=…)param descriptiontells the model each param's meaning
Field(ge=…, le=…)minimum / maximumconstrain the value range
has a default?requiredno default → required
⚠️ Approval mode
approval_mode="never_require" is just for sample brevity — in production use "always_require" to require human confirmation before a tool runs (especially for writes / spend).
1 Auto schema generation click to expand
🧪 Example
from typing import Annotated
from agent_framework import tool
from pydantic import Field

@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def search_products(
    query: Annotated[str, Field(description="Search keywords")],
    max_results: Annotated[int, Field(description="Max results to return", ge=1, le=10)] = 5
) -> list[dict]:
    """Search for products in the catalog."""
    ...

# Auto-generated JSON Schema:
# {
#   "name": "search_products",
#   "description": "Search for products in the catalog.",
#   "parameters": {
#     "type": "object",
#     "properties": {
#       "query": {"type": "string", "description": "Search keywords"},
#       "max_results": {"type": "integer", "description": "Max results...", "minimum": 1, "maximum": 10, "default": 5}
#     },
#     "required": ["query"]
#   }
# }
❓ Why this matters
Models need JSON Schema to understand tool signatures: parameter names, types, constraints, meanings. Problems with hand-written schemas:
  • Error-prone: field name typos, type mismatches, forgotten description updates.
  • Double maintenance: function signature changes, schema must sync, easy to miss.
  • No type checking: schema and code diverge, wrong arguments only caught at runtime.
Auto-generation makes type annotations = single source of truth, function signature is the contract.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF uses Pydantic to auto-generate schemas (see FunctionTool in python/packages/core/agent_framework/_tools.py):
  • Type annotations → type field (str → "string", int → "integer"…).
  • Field(description=...) → description field.
  • Field(..., ge=1, le=10) → minimum/maximum constraints.
  • Default values → default field, optional parameters.
  • Docstring → tool's description.
IDE autocomplete, type checkers (mypy) validate calls, schema and code always in sync.
🔀 Alternatives
  • Hand-written JSON Schema: early OpenAI SDK approach, error-prone, high maintenance.
  • LangChain @tool: also uses Pydantic, but weak support for complex types (nested objects, unions).
  • OpenAI function calling JSON: manual construction of {"name": ..., "parameters": {...}}, no type safety.
  • MAF fully automatic: supports generics, nesting, optional params, constraints; generated schema matches all vendor specs.
2 Approval modes click to expand
🧪 Example
@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def get_time() -> str:
    """Get current time (safe, always auto-execute)."""
    return datetime.now().isoformat()

@tool(approval_mode="always_require")
def send_email(to: str, subject: str, body: str) -> str:
    """Send an email (requires human approval)."""
    ...  # framework requests confirmation before execution

@tool(approval_mode="always_require")
def create_ticket(title: str, priority: str) -> str:
    """Create a ticket (low-priority auto, high requires approval)."""
    ...  # can customize risk assessment logic
❓ Why this matters
Tools vary in capability and risk:
  • Read-only queries (check weather, search): no side effects, can auto-execute.
  • Write operations (send email, delete files, transfer money): have side effects, must require human confirmation.
  • Gray area (create draft, add note): low-risk can auto, high-risk needs confirmation.
If all tools auto-execute (never_require), one model hallucination could send wrong emails, delete wrong data. If all tools require confirmation (always_require), UX is terrible (confirming even weather lookups). Need tiered control.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's approval_mode (see python/packages/core/agent_framework/_tools.py):
  • "never_require": no confirmation, auto-execute (default).
  • "always_require": pop confirmation dialog / callback every time, execute only after user approval.
Approval flow defined by approval logic interface (see _tools.py), customizable (e.g., integrate Slack approval bot, DingTalk approval flow). Framework auto-intercepts before tool execution, no need to write approval logic inside tool functions.
🔀 Alternatives
  • LangChain HumanApprovalCallbackHandler: implemented via callbacks, but must manually register to each Agent, easy to miss.
  • Manual checks in tools: if dangerous: ask_user(); ..., couples approval logic with business logic.
  • OpenAI function calling: no built-in approval, application layer must intercept function_call and handle.
  • MAF declarative approval: one line @tool(approval_mode=...), approval logic decoupled from tools, pluggable strategies.
3 Async tools click to expand
🧪 Example
import httpx

# Sync tool (blocks event loop)
@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def get_weather_sync(city: str) -> str:
    """Get weather (blocks event loop)."""
    resp = httpx.get(f"https://api.weather.com?q={city}")  # blocking!
    return resp.json()["temp"]

# Async tool (non-blocking)
@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
async def get_weather_async(city: str) -> str:
    """Get weather (async, doesn't block)."""
    async with httpx.AsyncClient() as client:
        resp = await client.get(f"https://api.weather.com?q={city}")
        return resp.json()["temp"]

# Agent auto-handles: sync via run_in_executor, async via direct await
❓ Why this matters
Tools often do I/O:
  • HTTP requests: calling third-party APIs (weather, maps, payments).
  • Database queries: reading user info, order history.
  • File I/O: reading config, writing logs.
Synchronous I/O blocks the event loop: one tool call taking 2 seconds freezes the entire Agent for 2 seconds, can't handle other requests. Async I/O lets the event loop continue, multiple tool calls can execute concurrently (one waiting on network while another runs CPU tasks). Python's asyncio and .NET's Task both require async contagion — async all the way.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's @tool supports both sync and async (see FunctionTool.invoke in python/packages/core/agent_framework/_tools.py):
  • Sync tools: framework uses asyncio.to_thread (or run_in_executor) to run in thread pool, avoiding blocking.
  • Async tools: framework directly awaits, doesn't block event loop.
  • Auto-detection: inspect.iscoroutinefunction(...) determines if async.
  • Concurrent execution: multiple tool calls can use asyncio.gather for concurrency (future support for parallel tool calls).
Developers don't care about sync/async details; framework ensures efficient execution.
🔀 Alternatives
  • LangChain: @tool supports async, but AgentExecutor's sync/async paths separated, easy to get wrong.
  • OpenAI SDK: doesn't care if tools are sync/async, application layer handles it.
  • Sync only: simple but poor performance, Agent handling concurrent requests will block.
  • MAF transparent handling: write sync tools (simple) or async tools (efficient), framework auto-optimizes.
4 Multiple tools together click to expand
🧪 Example
@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def search_docs(query: str) -> list[str]:
    """Search documentation for relevant pages."""
    ...

@tool(approval_mode="never_require")
def read_page(page_id: str) -> str:
    """Read the full content of a documentation page."""
    ...

@tool(approval_mode="always_require")
def create_ticket(title: str, description: str) -> str:
    """Create a support ticket."""
    ...

agent = Agent(
    client=client,
    name="SupportBot",
    instructions="Help users find docs or file tickets.",
    tools=[search_docs, read_page, create_ticket]
)

# User: "How do I reset my password?"
# Model: calls search_docs("reset password") → gets [page_123, page_456]
# Model: calls read_page("page_123") → reads steps
# Model: answers user

# User: "I still can't log in, please help"
# Model: calls create_ticket(...) → framework pops confirmation → user approves → ticket created
❓ Why this matters
Real Agents need multiple capabilities:
  • Retrieval: search knowledge base, database, API.
  • Read/write: read files, write logs, update records.
  • Computation: call calculator, run code.
  • Interaction: send emails, create tickets, call third-party services.
Single tools are limited; multiple tools combined complete complex tasks. Model needs to plan: search first → then read → finally create ticket. Framework needs to support multi-round tool calls (one tool's output may trigger the next).
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's tool loop (see _run_non_streaming in python/packages/core/agent_framework/_agents.py):
  • Pass all tool schemas: on Agent init, all tools' JSON Schemas sent to model.
  • Model selects tools: model decides which tool(s) to call based on user question, outputs function_call.
  • Framework routes execution: based on function_call.name, finds corresponding Python function, calls with args.
  • Feed back results: appends function_result to conversation history; model reads and decides: call more tools or give final answer.
  • Loop until done: max max_tool_rounds (default 10), prevents infinite loops.
Developers just define tools, pass them to Agent; scheduling and composition done by framework.
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI parallel_tool_calls: supports returning multiple tool_calls at once, MAF will support in future (concurrent tool execution).
  • Anthropic tool_use blocks: one message can contain multiple tool_use, MAF already abstracts in Content.from_function_call.
  • LangChain ToolChain: must manually orchestrate tool call order (A → B → C), less flexible than letting model plan.
  • MAF auto-loop + model planning: provide tools, model composes them, framework ensures safe execution (approval + round limits).
✅ Key points
  • @tool turns a plain function into a model-callable tool; the schema is auto-generated.
  • Use Annotated[..., Field(description=…)] for params and a docstring for purpose.
  • Put tools in Agent(tools=[…]); execution + feedback is handled by the framework.
💡 Design highlight
"A function is a tool": you never hand-write JSON Schema — type hints and the docstring are the contract. Code and the model-facing description can't drift, because they're the same thing.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. After decorating a plain function with @tool, where does the tool's JSON Schema (handed to the model) come from?
  1. Auto-generated from the signature + type hints (Annotated[…, Field(description=…)]) + docstring
  2. You must hand-write a JSON Schema string
  3. The model guesses the parameters itself
  4. Read from an external database
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. @tool reads the signature / hints / docstring to emit the JSON Schema for the model: the docstring becomes the tool description, Field(description=…) inside Annotated becomes parameter docs, and defaults/constraints carry over. So "writing good types and a docstring" is writing the model's instruction manual.
2. What does approval_mode="always_require" on a @tool mean?
  1. Auto-approves every call
  2. Never calls this tool
  3. Pause before the tool runs and require human confirmation (human-in-the-loop) - good for write / spend actions
  4. Makes the tool run faster
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. ApprovalMode is Literal["always_require", "never_require"]. It makes "needs human review?" a declarative parameter on the tool rather than scattered through business logic - risky actions (publish, delete, pay) should default to always_require, and the framework pauses for confirmation before running.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Using "a plain Python function + one @tool decorator" as a tool - versus a dedicated tool-description language / config file - what are the developer-experience upsides and the hidden risks? (Hint: what happens if the docstring or type hints are sloppy?)
Part 2 · User's View

07 · Sessions & Memory

By default each run() is "amnesiac". To make an Agent remember, pass the same session (AgentSession) across calls. For longer-term memory, use a ContextProvider.

📒 Analogy
An AgentSession is a shared notebook: every turn writes into the same book, so next turn the model "remembers" by reading it. A ContextProvider is an assistant that quietly slips "relevant old memories / external knowledge" into the context before each answer.

The memory stack: three layers

short-termAgentSession
Conversation history for one session: pass the same session to every run and history accumulates.
injectContextProvider
Context pipeline: before_run() injects relevant memory / retrieved context, after_run() writes back.
backendHistoryProvider / MemoryStore
Where data actually lives: in-memory / file / vector store / Redis / Cosmos — pluggable.

Multi-turn: carry a session

samples/01-get-started/03_multi_turn.py
session = agent.create_session()

result = await agent.run(
    "My name is Alice and I love hiking.", session=session)

result = await agent.run(
    "What do you remember about me?", session=session)
# → remembers Alice, likes hiking

The whole trick: pass the same session into every run() and history accumulates. Omit the session and each call starts fresh.

Longer-term memory / external knowledge

🔬 ContextProvider
A session only accumulates within "this conversation". To remember user preferences across sessions, or to inject retrieved knowledge (RAG) before answering, use a ContextProvider — e.g. MemoryContextProvider stores memories in a searchable store and splices relevant entries into context before each run. (Memory stores / backends: Lesson 16 and the provider packages.)

One run, with memory

1

before_run() injects

Before the model call, each ContextProvider adds session history / retrieved memory into the SessionContext.

2

Assemble & call the LLM

The Agent sends the injected context + this turn's input to the model and gets a reply.

3

after_run() writes back

Once the answer is produced, the provider's after_run() stores the new messages / extracted facts back to the backend for next time.

Two kinds of memory

DimensionShort-term: session historyLong-term: provider memory
CarrierAgentSessionMemoryContextProvider, etc.
Scopewithin one sessionacross sessions / users
Storagemessage list inside the sessionvector store / database (searchable)
How injectedpass the session; history rides alongbefore_run() retrieves then injects
Typical use"and tomorrow?" continuityuser preferences / RAG retrieval
1 With vs without session click to expand
🧪 Example
# Without session: each call is a new conversation (amnesiac)
result1 = await agent.run("I'm Alice")
result2 = await agent.run("What's my name?")
# → Model doesn't know; previous "Alice" is lost

# With session: remembers context
session = agent.create_session()
result1 = await agent.run("I'm Alice", session=session)
result2 = await agent.run("What's my name?", session=session)
# → Model answers "Alice"

# View session history
print(session.history)  # [Message("user", "I'm Alice"), Message("assistant", ...), ...]
❓ Why this matters
Many AI apps need context continuity:
  • Conversational UI: user asks "What's the weather?" → "Tomorrow?" → "Day after?", each relies on prior turns.
  • Task flows: user says "Book me a hotel" → model asks "Which city?" → "Seattle" → "Check-in date?", needs to maintain state.
  • Personalization: user says "I'm vegetarian", later conversations remember this.
If default is stateful (implicit global session), you get: different users' conversations mixed, tests hard to isolate, concurrent requests interfere. MAF chooses stateless as default, explicit session when needed.
✅ How MAF does it
AgentSession (see python/packages/core/agent_framework/_sessions.py):
  • Explicit creation: agent.create_session() returns a new session object.
  • Pass-through: pass the same session object to each run().
  • Auto-accumulation: after each run, user message + model reply (+ tool calls/results) auto-append to session.history.
  • No side effects: without session, run mutates no global state, fully stateless.
This design ensures concurrent safety (each user's session doesn't interfere), testability (each test has independent session), understandability (memory or not is obvious).
🔀 Alternatives
  • OpenAI Assistants API: uses thread_id to identify sessions, server-managed history. Pros: no client storage. Cons: can't debug locally, vendor switch requires refactor.
  • LangChain Memory: uses ConversationBufferMemory classes, must manually attach to Chain. Easy to forget to clear, causing history bloat.
  • Implicit global session: framework internally maintains session dict (user_id → history), simplifies API but concurrency-unsafe, hard to test.
  • MAF explicit session object: clear, safe, controllable.
2 ContextProvider workflow click to expand
🧪 Example
from agent_framework import Agent, MemoryContextProvider

# Create a memory provider: injects relevant memories before each run
memory_provider = MemoryContextProvider(
    store=...,  # MemoryStore: the memory storage backend (vector DB / Redis / etc.)
)

agent = Agent(
    client=client,
    name="Assistant",
    context_providers=[memory_provider],  # attach
)

# User first says "I love hiking"
session = agent.create_session()
await agent.run("I love hiking", session=session)
# → MemoryContextProvider stores "user loves hiking" in memory bank

# Days later, new session
session2 = agent.create_session()
await agent.run("Suggest weekend activities", session=session2)
# → MemoryContextProvider retrieves "loves hiking", injects into context
# → Model answers considering this memory
❓ Why this matters
AgentSession only remembers within a single conversation; after it ends, history is gone. Real apps need cross-session memory:
  • User preferences: user says "I'm allergic to seafood", all future conversations must remember.
  • RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation): user asks "Company vacation policy?", retrieve relevant doc paragraphs, inject into context.
  • Personalization: based on user interaction history (questions asked, preferences stated), adjust answer style.
Hard-coding these into instructions or manually splicing into each message is tedious and non-extensible. Need pluggable context injection.
✅ How MAF does it
ContextProvider interface (see python/packages/core/agent_framework/):
  • Called before each run: Agent execution iterates through context_providers list, calls each provider's before_run(...).
  • Injects context: in before_run the provider adds Messages (history), Content (doc snippets), instructions, or tools to the SessionContext.
  • Built-in implementations: MemoryContextProvider (vector retrieval memory), InMemoryHistoryProvider (cross-session history), FileHistoryProvider (file-backed history).
  • Composable: multiple providers execute in order, can use memory + doc retrieval + user preferences together.
This design decouples context logic; Agent doesn't care "where memory comes from", only "have this context".
🔀 Alternatives
  • LangChain Memory: ConversationBufferMemory, ConversationSummaryMemory, etc., but coupled to Chain, not easy to swap storage backends.
  • LlamaIndex chat memory: focuses on vector retrieval, but doesn't support other context types (user preferences, doc snippets).
  • Manual splicing: before run, manually query memory bank, splice into message list — repetitive code, error-prone.
  • MAF interface abstraction + built-ins: out-of-box providers, can also custom (e.g., integrate enterprise knowledge graph).
3 Session storage backends click to expand
🧪 Example
# Dev environment: in-memory storage (lost on restart)
from agent_framework import InMemoryHistoryProvider
history = InMemoryHistoryProvider()

# Production: Redis storage (persistent + distributed)
from agent_framework.redis import RedisHistoryProvider
history = RedisHistoryProvider(redis_url="redis://localhost:6379")

# Azure: Cosmos DB storage
from agent_framework.azure import CosmosHistoryProvider
history = CosmosHistoryProvider(
    endpoint="https://...",
    credential="...",
    database_name="agents",
    container_name="sessions"
)

# Agent uses the provider (injected as a context provider)
agent = Agent(client=client, context_providers=[history])
session = agent.create_session(session_id="user_123_conv_456")
# History auto-saved to backend, restored on next load
❓ Why this matters
Different environments have different storage needs:
  • Dev / test: in-memory storage suffices, fast, no config.
  • Single-server production: file storage (SQLite, JSON), simple persistence.
  • Distributed production: Redis / Cosmos / DynamoDB, multi-instance state sharing, high availability.
  • Compliance / audit: must store in enterprise DB (PostgreSQL / MongoDB), easy to query, archive.
If storage logic is coupled to Agent (hard-coded Redis), switching environments requires code changes, hard to test. Need abstract storage interface.
✅ How MAF does it
HistoryProvider interface (subclass of ContextProvider, see python/packages/core/agent_framework/_sessions.py):
  • Abstract interface: subclasses implement get_messages / save_messages, Agent depends on it via context_providers.
  • Multiple implementations:
    • InMemoryHistoryProvider: for dev, in-memory storage.
    • RedisHistoryProvider: for production, Redis storage (agent_framework.redis).
    • CosmosHistoryProvider: for Azure users, Cosmos DB storage (agent_framework.azure).
    • FileHistoryProvider: single-server, JSON file storage.
  • Auto-serialization: Message.to_dict() converts to JSON, stores in backend; on load from_dict restores.
  • Extensible: custom backends (e.g., integrate PostgreSQL, MongoDB) just subclass HistoryProvider.
🔀 Alternatives
  • LangChain: RedisChatMessageHistory, FileChatMessageHistory, etc., but coupled to Memory classes, switching storage requires changing Memory init.
  • OpenAI Assistants: server-managed, developers can't choose storage backend, can't debug locally.
  • Manual serialization: write own json.dump(history, file), repetitive code, easy to get wrong (forget handling tool calls etc.).
  • MAF interface + multiple impls: dev uses memory, production switches to Redis, just one line config change.
4 Context window compaction click to expand
🧪 Example
from agent_framework import (
    CompactionProvider,
    SlidingWindowStrategy,
    SummarizationStrategy,
    ContextWindowCompactionStrategy,
)
