Part 4 · Advanced

17 · Observability Deep-Dive

In Lesson 14 you met "streaming + the span tree"—observability seen from the streaming angle. This lesson flips it: with observability as the lead, it shows how Agent Framework turns every run into queryable trace / metric / log, and how in production you use it to pinpoint latency, failures and cost.

🚗 Analogy
A trace is like a dashcam: ignored day to day, but after an incident you rewind frame by frame to see "which step, how long, who moved first". Metrics are like dashboard gauges (avg speed, fuel use); logs are the mechanic's written notes. Together they take you from "it feels slower" to "the 2nd chat call was 800ms slow".

The three pillars of observability

OpenTelemetry (OTel) splits "observability" into three signal types; Agent Framework instruments all three out of the box:

Tracespans
A run's invoke_agent → chat → execute_tool span tree: per-step duration, parent/child call relationships, which step threw (error.type). Answers "where is it slow".
Metrichistograms
gen_ai.client.token.usage, gen_ai.client.operation.duration, agent_framework.function.invocation.duration: aggregated latency distribution, token usage, failure rate. Answers "overall health".
Logevents
Structured logs and events. Sensitive data like message content and tool args is not recorded by default; it needs an explicit enable_sensitive_data (test/dev only). Answers "what exactly happened".

One line to wire telemetry to a backend

configure_otel_providers()
call once at startup
→
framework instrumentation
Agent/Chat/Tool auto span+metric
→
exporter
OTLP → :4317
→
backend
Jaeger / Azure Monitor…

You just call once at startup: configure_otel_providers(). After that the framework's AgentTelemetryLayer / ChatTelemetryLayer automatically attach spans and metrics to every run / get_response / tool call, exporting over OTLP to your configured backend. No hand-written instrumentation in business code.

Worked example: the span tree from one run

Same "What's the weather in Paris?", but this time we watch only the observability line (streaming details are in Lesson 14). A tool-using run grows this span tree:

1

Open the root span: invoke_agent

The moment agent.run("What's the weather in Paris?") enters, the framework opens the root span invoke_agent WeatherAgent (AGENT_INVOKE_OPERATION="invoke_agent", observability.py:294), with attribute gen_ai.agent.name.

2

Child span: first chat, the model decides to call a tool

A child span chat gpt-4o opens (CHAT_COMPLETION_OPERATION="chat", :289), with gen_ai.request.model=gpt-4o and eventually gen_ai.response.finish_reasons=[tool_calls].

3

One level deeper: execute_tool nests inside chat

The tool-execution span execute_tool get_weather (:291) hangs under the chat span— the source parents "inner tool execution" under this chat span (observability.py:1556). Attributes: gen_ai.tool.name, gen_ai.tool.call.id.

4

Second chat: produce the final text with the tool result

Another chat gpt-4o span opens, this time with finish_reasons=[stop], recording gen_ai.usage.input_tokens / output_tokens.

5

Close bottom-up, emit metrics

Spans close bottom-up along the call stack, each recording an exact duration; metrics fire alongside: gen_ai.client.token.usage, gen_ai.client.operation.duration, agent_framework.function.invocation.duration. The root span also rolls up token usage from the inner chats.

The tree looks like this—child durations roll up into the parent, so you can see at a glance where the time went:

invoke_agent WeatherAgent              # root: total 1.9s
└─ chat gpt-4o                         # first model call → tool_calls
   └─ execute_tool get_weather         # tool exec (nested in chat) 120ms
└─ chat gpt-4o                         # second: text with result → stop
spanoperationkey attributes
invoke_agent WeatherAgentinvoke_agentgen_ai.agent.name · usage.* (rolled up)
chat gpt-4ochatgen_ai.request.model · response.finish_reasons
execute_tool get_weatherexecute_toolgen_ai.tool.name · gen_ai.tool.call.id

Real source: wire up OTel once

observability.pyconfigure_otel_providers (simplified from :1151)
from agent_framework.observability import configure_otel_providers

# Call once at app startup, and only once (before any telemetry is captured)
configure_otel_providers(
    enable_sensitive_data=False,       # record messages/args etc.? test/dev only
    enable_console_exporters=False,    # print trace/metric/log to console (local debug)
    exporters=None,                    # add custom OTLP exporters
    views=None,                        # metric views: filter/trim which metrics to collect
    vs_code_extension_port=None,       # attach VS Code AI Toolkit / Foundry extension
)
# Or configure entirely via env vars, e.g.:
#   OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://localhost:4317
#   ENABLE_CONSOLE_EXPORTERS=true

Note it's keyword-only (the * in the signature) and the docs say "call once". It reads standard OTel env vars (OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT, etc.), so usually you pass nothing and configure via the environment. For finer control over switches, use ObservabilitySettings (:666), which maps to env vars like ENABLE_INSTRUMENTATION / ENABLE_SENSITIVE_DATA / ENABLE_CONSOLE_EXPORTERS.

1 Why bake instrumentation into the framework instead of making you write it click to expand
🧪 Example
You write zero instrumentation code, yet agent.run(...) auto-emits an invoke_agent span; swap the ChatClient or add a tool and the span tree follows. The only switch is one configure_otel_providers() at startup.
❓ Why it's necessary
Hand-written instrumentation is verbose and easy to miss—and the un-instrumented path is exactly the one that breaks. It also tangles with business logic over time. Framework-level instrumentation = complete coverage, consistent style, one-switch control.
✅ MAF's approach & benefits
Instrumentation lives in layers like AgentTelemetryLayer / ChatTelemetryLayer (the same "onion" idea as middleware), transparent to your Agent/tool code. Attributes follow the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions (gen_ai.*), so any compatible backend understands them directly.
🔀 Alternatives
Each vendor SDK's private logging (different formats, can't correlate across vendors), or wrapping your own trace layer (reinventing the wheel). OTel standard + built-in instrumentation is the "write once, observable everywhere" path.
2 What it actually pinpoints in production click to expand
🧪 Example
"Users say it got slower"—open the trace, see invoke_agent is 1.9s with 1.4s in the second chat; check metrics, operation.duration p95 is indeed up; the problem is on the model side, not the tool.
❓ Why it's necessary
Agents are distributed, async, multi-step: one answer chains a model + several tools + retrieval. Without traces, "which step is slow/failing/expensive" is guesswork; with a span tree, causality is obvious.
✅ MAF's approach & benefits
Latency: span durations and operation.duration; failures: spans carrying error.type; cost: gen_ai.usage.input_tokens/output_tokens and the token.usage metric, attributable per agent/model. One signal set covers all three.
🔀 Alternatives
App logs only (no cross-step causality, hard to aggregate), or vendor billing only (totals, not attributable to a specific agent). Trace + metric together answer both "which step" and "overall trend".
✅ Key points
  • Three pillars: trace (span tree · which step), metric (aggregate · overall trend), log (events · what happened).
  • Spans are named invoke_agent / chat / execute_tool; the tool span nests inside the chat span, and durations roll up into the parent.
  • Call configure_otel_providers() once at startup (keyword-only); configure the rest via OTel env vars; instrumentation is done by the framework's telemetry layers.
  • Sensitive data (messages/args) is off by default and needs enable_sensitive_data; attributes follow the gen_ai.* semantic conventions.
💡 Design highlight
Observability is a layer, not scattered logs. Instrumentation is folded into AgentTelemetryLayer/ChatTelemetryLayer, wrapping the call like middleware— your Agent code has no idea it's being observed, yet emits a complete trace conforming to the OpenTelemetry GenAI conventions. On or off is a single line at startup.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. For the span tree from a tool-using run(), which matches the framework's actual behavior?
  1. Just one flat invoke_agent span with no children
  2. invoke_agent is the root span, chat is its child, and tool execution execute_tool nests inside the chat span; child durations roll up into the parent
  3. execute_tool is the root and invoke_agent is its child
  4. Spans are all siblings with no parent/child relationship
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. The framework opens root invoke_agent {agent}, then chat {model}; the source parents inner tool execution under that chat span (observability.py:1556), so execute_tool nests inside chat. A span tree rolls child durations into parents, which is how you locate "which step is slow".
2. The standard way to wire OpenTelemetry into Agent Framework in production is?
  1. Hand-write span open/close code inside every tool function
  2. You must fork the framework source to add instrumentation
  3. Re-initialize OTel before and after every run()
  4. Call configure_otel_providers() once at startup (keyword-only), configure the rest via OTel env vars; the framework's telemetry layers instrument automatically
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. configure_otel_providers() (observability.py:1151) is documented to be called once, before any telemetry, and reads standard OTel env vars. After that, AgentTelemetryLayer/ChatTelemetryLayer attach spans+metrics to every call—zero instrumentation in business code.
3. Among the three pillars, metrics are best suited to answer which kind of question?
  1. The model's internal weight values
  2. Aggregate trends: overall latency distribution (p95), token usage, failure rate
  3. In this single call, exactly how much slower step 2 was than step 1
  4. The verbatim content of one specific message
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. Traces answer "single run, which step" (span tree); metrics answer "aggregate trend" (histograms like gen_ai.client.operation.duration, token.usage); logs answer "what exactly happened" (events, sensitive content opt-in). The three are complementary.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Suppose a production Agent is "occasionally slow". Using this lesson's three pillars, design an investigation path: which trace spans would you look at first, which metrics next, and when would you actually need to flip enable_sensitive_data to inspect logs? Also explain why defaulting sensitive data off is a reasonable tradeoff.
Part 5 · Build Your Own

18 · Model Providers

MAF's ChatClient is vendor-agnostic — but you pick a concrete vendor to connect a model. This lesson surveys the main provider packages and shows the import-to-run path.

🔌 Analogy
ChatClient is a universal socket; each provider package is a country-specific plug — pick the Foundry, OpenAI or Anthropic plug, snap it into the socket, and you're charging.

Key Providers

ProviderPackageChatClient class
Azure AI Foundryagent-framework-foundryFoundryChatClient
OpenAI / Azure OpenAIagent-framework (built-in)OpenAIChatClient
Anthropic Claudeagent-framework-anthropicAnthropicClient
Ollama (local)agent-framework-ollamaOllamaChatClient
AWS Bedrockagent-framework-bedrockBedrockChatClient

Same pattern for all: instantiate ChatClient → client.as_agent(…) → agent.run(…).

From import to run: one path for every provider

import
pick a provider package
→
instantiate
XxxChatClient(model=…)
→
.as_agent(…)
vendor-agnostic
→
agent.run(…)
vendor-agnostic

Only the first two boxes change per vendor (package name + ChatClient class); the last two are identical for every provider. That's the "universal socket": swap the plug, keep the appliance - switch OpenAI for Anthropic and the downstream Agent code doesn't change a line.

1 FoundryChatClient configuration expand
🧪 Example
Full setup code: 
from agent_framework_foundry import FoundryChatClient
from azure.identity import DefaultAzureCredential
import os

client = FoundryChatClient(
    project_endpoint=os.environ["AZURE_AI_FOUNDRY_PROJECT_ENDPOINT"],
    model="gpt-4o",
    credential=DefaultAzureCredential()
)
❓ Why this matters
Azure AI Foundry unifies model management, memory, and file storage under one endpoint. Enterprise customers already have Foundry projects, so they can reuse existing infrastructure and security configurations.
✅ How MAF does it
FoundryChatClient takes three parameters: project_endpoint, model, credential. One import, three params, done.
🔀 Alternatives
Direct REST calls to Foundry API, or Azure SDK directly. MAF wraps it into the ChatClient interface so you can swap providers.
2 OpenAIChatClient configuration expand
🧪 Example
Works with both OpenAI and Azure OpenAI: 
from agent_framework.openai import OpenAIChatClient

# OpenAI
client = OpenAIChatClient(
    model="gpt-4o",
    api_key=os.environ["OPENAI_API_KEY"]
)

# Azure OpenAI
client = OpenAIChatClient(
    model="gpt-4o",
    base_url="https://your-resource.openai.azure.com/",
    api_key=os.environ["AZURE_OPENAI_KEY"]
)
❓ Why this matters
OpenAI is the most common starting point. Azure OpenAI adds enterprise security (VNet, managed identity). Many teams prototype with OpenAI, then migrate to Azure OpenAI.
✅ How MAF does it
Built into core package (no extra install). Works with both OpenAI and Azure OpenAI via base_url. Identical interface.
🔀 Alternatives
openai SDK directly, LiteLLM. MAF's advantage: same interface as all other providers.
3 AnthropicClient configuration expand
🧪 Example
Install package, then configure: 
# Install
pip install agent-framework-anthropic

# Use
from agent_framework_anthropic import AnthropicClient

client = AnthropicClient(
    model="claude-sonnet-4-5",
    api_key=os.environ["ANTHROPIC_API_KEY"]
)
# Also: claude-haiku-3-5, etc.
❓ Why this matters
Claude models excel at long context and careful reasoning. Many teams want provider diversity to reduce risk, compare quality, or work around limits from one vendor.
✅ How MAF does it
Separate package keeps core lightweight. Same as_agent() → run() pattern. Agent code identical to OpenAI version.
🔀 Alternatives
anthropic SDK directly, AWS Bedrock (which also supports Claude). MAF unifies the interface.
4 OllamaChatClient configuration expand
🧪 Example
Run Ollama locally, no API key needed: 
# 1. Install Ollama (locally) and pull model
ollama pull llama3.1

# 2. Python code
from agent_framework_ollama import OllamaChatClient

client = OllamaChatClient(
    model="llama3.1",
    endpoint="http://localhost:11434"  # default
)
❓ Why this matters
Local models = no API costs, no data leaving your machine. Great for development and privacy-sensitive use cases.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF treats local models identically to cloud ones. Same agent code works with Ollama or GPT-4o, just swap the client.
🔀 Alternatives
vLLM, llama.cpp server, LocalAI. Ollama is simplest for development, but MAF's abstraction means you can write other local backend ChatClients.
5 Switching providers demo expand
🧪 Example
Same Agent definition, only swap the client line: 
# Variant 1: OpenAI
client = OpenAIChatClient(model="gpt-4o", api_key=...)
agent = client.as_agent(
    name="Assistant",
    instructions="You are helpful.",
    tools=[web_search]
)

# Variant 2: Anthropic
client = AnthropicClient(model="claude-sonnet-4-5", api_key=...)
agent = client.as_agent(
    name="Assistant",
    instructions="You are helpful.",
    tools=[web_search]
)

# Variant 3: Ollama
client = OllamaChatClient(model="llama3.1")
agent = client.as_agent(
    name="Assistant",
    instructions="You are helpful.",
    tools=[web_search]
)
# Agent definition (name, instructions, tools, middleware) identical!
❓ Why this matters
Vendor lock-in is expensive. Being able to switch with one line change is a competitive advantage: switch when prices change, when one vendor rate-limits you, or to compare quality.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's ChatClient abstraction means agent logic is 100% provider-independent. Only the client instantiation changes. Tools, middleware, orchestration all stay the same.
🔀 Alternatives
LiteLLM proxy (adds a network hop), LangChain's ChatModel (similar idea but heavier). MAF is the cleanest abstraction.
✅ Key points
  • One provider package per vendor, each supplying a ChatClient subclass.
  • Unified pattern: instantiate → as_agent() → run().
  • Switching vendors changes only the import and instantiation lines.
💡 Design highlight
Adding a vendor never touches core: providers are separate packages, wired in via lazy loading. The community can build new providers without forking the repo.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. Why does switching the model vendor from OpenAI to Anthropic usually touch only the one or two "instantiate the ChatClient" lines, leaving downstream Agent code unchanged?
  1. Because the Agent code is actually hard-wired to OpenAI
  2. The as_agent() / run() layer is vendor-agnostic; each provider package only adapts its own API to the same ChatClient abstraction
  3. Because every vendor's HTTP API format is already identical
  4. Because MAF auto-translates your prompt at runtime
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. ChatClient is an abstraction boundary: each provider package implements the same interface and the Agent depends only on the abstraction. So everything above "construct the ChatClient" varies by vendor and everything below is reused - switching vendors is a local change.
2. MAF uses one OpenAIChatClient for both OpenAI and Azure OpenAI (distinguished by base_url / credentials) instead of two classes. Why?
  1. They share the same underlying wire protocol and differ only in endpoint / auth - one parameterized class is easier to maintain than two near-duplicate classes
  2. Because Azure OpenAI has no SDK of its own
  3. Because OpenAI and Azure are the same company
  4. Because MAF doesn't actually support Azure OpenAI
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. The two deployments speak the same protocol and differ only in endpoint and credential. One parameterized class beats maintaining two near-identical ones - and it echoes "vendor-agnostic": even one vendor's two deployments converge on the same interface.
3. When connecting Anthropic Claude, what is the real ChatClient class name in MAF?
  1. ClaudeChatClient
  2. AnthropicAgent
  3. AnthropicClient
  4. AnthropicChatClient
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. The real class is AnthropicClient (not AnthropicChatClient), imported from agent_framework.anthropic; OpenAI's is the one called OpenAIChatClient. Naming isn't perfectly uniform - use the real name from the package rather than assuming a *ChatClient suffix.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Using the real class names, write the import + instantiation lines for "connect OpenAI" and "connect Anthropic". Then say, for an already-written agent.run(...) business function that must switch between the two, which lines change and which stay identical - and which design principle explains that.
Part 5 · Build Your Own

19 · Declarative Agents (YAML)

Don't want to write code? Define an Agent in YAML — name, instructions, tools and model all in a config file.

📝 Analogy
A declarative Agent is like a résumé: you list name, skills and experience; HR (the framework) reads it and onboards the person.

A YAML example

my_agent.yaml
name: WeatherBot
instructions: You help with weather queries.
model: gpt-4o
tools:
  - get_weather

The declarative package reads this YAML and auto-constructs an Agent. Great for rapid prototyping and version control.

From YAML to running, end to end

my_agent.yaml
name / instructions / tools
→
AgentFactory
create_agent_from_yaml_path
→
Agent instance
identical to hand-written
→
agent.run(…)
called as usual

Key insight: YAML is just another way to construct. What AgentFactory emits after parsing is the same kind of object you'd build by hand with Agent(client=…, tools=…) - so downstream run(), tools and middleware all work unchanged. Config and code are isomorphic, which is what lets you move freely between the two styles.

1 Complete YAML schema expand
🧪 Example
All supported fields: 
name: MyAgent
instructions: You are a helpful assistant.
model: gpt-4o
description: Optional agent description

tools:
  - tool_name_1
  - tool_name_2

middleware:
  - LoggingMiddleware
  - RetryMiddleware

context_providers:
  - provider_name
Each field explained: name (agent name), instructions (system prompt), model (model name), tools (tool list), middleware (middleware list), context_providers (context providers), description (optional description).
❓ Why this matters
Without knowing the full schema, users guess at field names and waste time debugging YAML typos. An explicit schema makes configuration predictable.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's declarative package validates the YAML against a known schema. Unknown fields raise clear errors instead of silently failing.
🔀 Alternatives
JSON Schema validation, Pydantic models for config. YAML is human-friendly and diff-friendly for version control.
2 Loading code expand
🧪 Example
Load from YAML to running: 
from agent_framework.declarative import AgentFactory

agent = AgentFactory().create_agent_from_yaml_path("my_agent.yaml")
result = await agent.run("Hello")
Just a few lines. AgentFactory.create_agent_from_yaml_path reads YAML → fully configured Agent instance.
❓ Why this matters
If loading is complex, declarative loses its appeal. It should be as easy as "load → run".
✅ How MAF does it
MAF makes it a one-liner: load YAML, get a fully configured Agent. The declarative package handles tool resolution, model wiring, etc.
🔀 Alternatives
Hydra (Facebook), OmegaConf. MAF's approach is purpose-built for agents, not generic config.
3 YAML + code hybrid expand
🧪 Example
YAML defines skeleton, code adds custom tools: 
# 1. Define tool in Python
from agent_framework import tool

@tool
def my_custom_tool(query: str) -> str:
    """My custom logic"""
    return f"Result for {query}"

# 2. YAML references tool by name
# my_agent.yaml:
#   tools:
#     - my_custom_tool

# 3. Inject Python tools by name via bindings
agent = AgentFactory(bindings={"my_custom_tool": my_custom_tool}).create_agent_from_yaml_path("my_agent.yaml")
Workflow: define tool in Python → reference by name in YAML → load.
❓ Why this matters
Real agents need custom logic that can't be expressed in YAML. Hybrid lets non-devs own the config while devs own the logic.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's tool registry means YAML can reference Python functions by name. Best of both worlds.
🔀 Alternatives
Full code (loses non-dev accessibility), full YAML with embedded code (security risk). Hybrid is the sweet spot.
4 Declarative workflows expand
🧪 Example
Can workflows (SequentialBuilder, ConcurrentBuilder) be defined in YAML? 
# Currently: declarative support is good for single agents
# Workflows typically need code for complex routing logic

# Possible future YAML (if supported):
workflow:
  participants:
    - writer_agent
    - reviewer_agent
  orchestration: sequential
Current limitation: complex routing logic in workflows is best expressed in code.
❓ Why this matters
If single agents can be declarative, teams will want declarative workflows too. It's the natural next step.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's declarative support is evolving. Currently best for single agents; workflows typically need code for complex routing logic. This is an active area of development.
🔀 Alternatives
Temporal workflow definitions, AWS Step Functions (JSON). Declarative workflows are an active area of development.
✅ Key points
  • YAML defines an Agent: name / instructions / model / tools.
  • Loaded by the declarative package; auto-constructs Agent.
  • Good for prototyping, version control, non-developer collaboration.
💡 Design highlight
"Config is an Agent": tweak YAML to change behavior, no code changes, no recompilation. This lowers the barrier for non-developers to participate in Agent design.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. How does an Agent built from YAML via AgentFactory compare to one you hand-build with Agent(client=…, tools=…)?
  1. It can only run via a special yaml_run()
  2. It's the same kind of object; downstream run() / tools / middleware all behave identically
  3. It's slower because it re-parses the YAML on every call
  4. It's a restricted "read-only" Agent that can't call tools
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. Declarative is just another construction path: AgentFactory parses and emits a standard Agent instance. Config and code are isomorphic, so the two styles are interchangeable and everything downstream is unchanged.
2. What's the main benefit of putting the Agent definition in YAML instead of hard-coding it in .py?
  1. YAML can define unlimited tools
  2. YAML runs faster than Python
  3. YAML can bypass tool approval
  4. Config becomes data: non-developers can edit it, it diffs in version control, and you can swap files per environment - all without touching code
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. Declarative decouples "what" from "how it runs". Config-as-data means it's auditable, versionable and editable by non-engineers; create_agent_from_yaml_path loads it in one line.
3. What is the real call to load an Agent from a YAML file in MAF?
  1. AgentFactory().create_agent_from_yaml_path("my_agent.yaml")
  2. AgentFactory.load_agent("my_agent.yaml")
  3. Agent.from_yaml("my_agent.yaml")
  4. declarative.parse("my_agent.yaml")
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. The real API is from agent_framework.declarative import AgentFactory, then create_agent_from_yaml_path(...) (not load_agent). AgentFactory(bindings={...}) also binds tool / client names in the YAML to real objects.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Your team wants PMs to edit an Agent's instructions without shipping a release. Explain how declarative YAML supports that workflow, and how tools: [get_weather] in the YAML becomes a callable tool (who resolves the name and binds it to the real function).
Part 5 · Build Your Own

20 · Writing Your Own Middleware

Lesson 11 explained what middleware is; this lesson teaches you to write your own — from scratch to working.

🧱 Analogy
Writing middleware is like adding a station to an assembly line: you can inspect products before the next station (logging), stamp labels after (billing), or send defects back for rework (retry) — without modifying the line itself.

Hands-on: retry middleware

custom ChatMiddleware
from agent_framework import ChatMiddleware, ChatContext

class RetryChatMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    def __init__(self, max_retries: int = 3):
        self.max_retries = max_retries

    async def process(self, context: ChatContext, call_next):
        for attempt in range(self.max_retries):
            try:
                await call_next()
                return
            except Exception:
                if attempt == self.max_retries - 1:
                    raise

Attach to an Agent: Agent(client=client, middleware=[RetryChatMiddleware(3)]).

A request travels through the middleware (onion model)

run()
request starts
→
Retry.before
before call_next()
→
Logging.before
log input
→
the real call
LLM / tool
→
Logging.after
log output / timing
→
Retry.after
retry on failure
→
result
context.result

await call_next() is the dividing line: code before it runs as the request goes "in", code after it runs as the response comes "out" - so one middleware wraps both ends (the onion skin). Earlier middleware in the list sits further outside: first in, last out.

1 Complete logging middleware expand
🧪 Example
Full code with timing: 
import logging
import time
from agent_framework import ChatMiddleware, ChatContext

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)

class LoggingChatMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    async def process(self, context: ChatContext, call_next):
        logger.info(f"Input: {context.messages}")
        start = time.monotonic()
        
        await call_next()
        
        duration = time.monotonic() - start
        logger.info(f"Output: {context.result}")
        logger.info(f"Duration: {duration:.2f}s")
~15 lines for complete logging middleware. Log input before call_next(), output and duration after.
❓ Why this matters
Logging is the #1 most common middleware. Without it, debugging production agents is blind. Can't see inputs, outputs, or timing means can't diagnose issues.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's middleware pattern makes logging trivial: wrap call_next() with before/after logic. Access context.messages for input, context.result for output.
🔀 Alternatives
Decorator-based logging, OTel auto-instrumentation. Middleware is explicit and composable.
2 Approval middleware expand
🧪 Example
ApprovalMiddleware checks condition before call_next(): 
class ApprovalMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    async def process(self, context: ChatContext, call_next):
        user_input = context.messages[-1].content
        
        if "publish" in user_input or "delete" in user_input:
            # Pause, wait for human confirmation (integrate with HITL)
            approved = await request_human_approval(user_input)
            if not approved:
                raise Exception("Action not approved")
        
        await call_next()
If message contains "publish" or "delete", pause and wait for human signature before continuing.
❓ Why this matters
Autonomous agents need guardrails. Certain actions (publish, delete, pay) should require human approval. Without approval, risk is uncontrolled.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF middleware can intercept any call. Combined with request_info for async human input, it creates a complete approval flow.
🔀 Alternatives
Separate approval service, webhook-based approval. Middleware keeps it in-process and composable.
3 Token billing middleware expand
🧪 Example
After await call_next(), read usage: 
class BillingMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    def __init__(self):
        self.total_cost = 0.0
    
    async def process(self, context: ChatContext, call_next):
        await call_next()
        
        usage = context.result.usage
        # Assume prices: input $0.01/1k, output $0.03/1k
        cost = (usage.input_tokens * 0.01 + 
                usage.output_tokens * 0.03) / 1000
        self.total_cost += cost
        logger.info(f"Cost: ${cost:.4f}, Total: ${self.total_cost:.4f}")
~12 lines for real-time cost tracking. Read context.result.usage, calculate cost, accumulate.
❓ Why this matters
LLM costs can explode. Per-request cost tracking enables budgets, alerts, and per-user billing. Without it, bills are a mystery.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF exposes token usage on context.result.usage after each call. Middleware can read it without modifying the response.
🔀 Alternatives
Provider dashboard billing, external proxy (e.g., Helicone). Middleware gives real-time, in-process tracking.
4 Middleware error handling expand
🧪 Example
try/except around await call_next(): 
class ErrorHandlingMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    async def process(self, context: ChatContext, call_next):
        try:
            await call_next()
        except Exception as e:
            logger.error(f"Call failed: {e}")
            # Optional: retry logic
            # Optional: return fallback response
            # Or re-raise
            raise
On failure: log error, optionally retry, optionally return fallback. Pattern: catch exception, log, then re-raise.
❓ Why this matters
LLM calls fail (rate limits, timeouts, bad responses). Without error handling middleware, failures crash the entire agent.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's pipeline propagates exceptions naturally. Middleware can catch, log, retry, or transform errors at any layer.
🔀 Alternatives
Circuit breaker libraries (tenacity), global error handlers. Middleware is per-layer and composable.
✅ Key points
  • Subclass *Middleware; implement process(self, context, call_next).
  • await call_next() takes no args; result is on context.result.
  • Attach via Agent(client=client, middleware=[…]) - one list can mix Chat / Function / Agent middleware; the framework routes each by its base class.
💡 Design highlight
Middleware is compositional: retries, logging, approvals — each implemented independently, assembled as needed, no interference.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. To attach Chat, Function and Agent middleware to one Agent, the correct way is?
  1. Build a separate Agent for each middleware kind
  2. An Agent can hold only one middleware
  3. Use three separate params: chat_middleware= / function_middleware= / agent_middleware=
  4. Put them all in one middleware=[...] list; the framework routes each to the right layer by its base class
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. Agent(...) / as_agent(...) takes a single middleware= param and routes by base class (ChatMiddleware / FunctionMiddleware / AgentMiddleware). One slot, mixable, more composable. (chat_middleware / function_middleware are actually decorators that mark plain functions, not constructor params.)
2. When does code after await call_next() run, and why is the result read from context.result instead of call_next()'s return value?
  1. It never runs; code after call_next() is dead code
  2. It runs before the request goes "in"
  3. It runs as the response travels "out", after the inner layers finish; the result lives on the shared context so both ends and multiple middleware read/write one state
  4. call_next() directly returns the final string
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. call_next() is the dividing line: before = inbound, after = outbound (the onion). Putting the result on context rather than a return value lets any layer read/modify one shared context (rewrite output, record timing…) and lets the argument-free call_next() chain the whole pipeline.
3. With middleware=[A, B], what is the execution order?
  1. A runs fully, then B runs from scratch
  2. The order is random
  3. A.before → B.before → real call → B.after → A.after (first-in, last-out; A is outermost)
  4. B.before → A.before → call → A.after → B.after
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. Earlier in the list = further outside (onion skin): first in, last out. Order changes semantics - e.g. whether "retry" wraps "logging" or vice versa decides if retried failures get logged twice - so order is explicit and controllable.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • You're adding a "billing" middleware that records each call's token usage and reports it. Say which base class you'd subclass, what you'd do before vs after call_next(), and how placing it earlier vs later in the middleware= list changes what it measures (e.g. whether extra calls from retries get counted).
Part 5 · Build Your Own

21 · Checkpointing, HITL & Durability

Production needs: save progress mid-run (checkpoints), human approval at critical steps (HITL), resume after crashes (durability). MAF makes all three first-class.

💾 Analogy
Checkpoints are game saves: save progress, crash, reload from save. HITL is an approval workflow: pause at a critical node, wait for a signature. DurableTask is cloud saves: saves aren't local — move to another machine and keep playing.

Checkpoints

Workflows auto-save at each superstep boundary. Default: InMemoryCheckpointStorage; swap to persistent storage (local FileCheckpointStorage or cloud CosmosCheckpointStorage) for production. After a crash, restart and resume from the latest save.

Human-in-the-Loop (HITL)

Tools can set approval_mode="always_require" to pause for human confirmation. At the workflow level, request_info lets a node pause, ask a question, and wait for a reply.

A timeline with saves and pauses

superstep 1
Writer finishes
→
💾 save
superstep boundary
→
⏸ HITL
approval / request_info
→
superstep 2
Reviewer runs
→
💥 crash
process dies
→
↺ resume
from latest save

Each concern owns a slice: checkpointing auto-saves at every superstep boundary; HITL pauses the flow at key nodes to wait for a human; the persistent backend (File / Cosmos) decides where saves live and whether you can recover after a crash. Note that the Redis package is for context / history memory, not workflow checkpoints.

1 InMemoryCheckpointStorage example expand
🧪 Example
Setup code: 
from agent_framework import InMemoryCheckpointStorage
from agent_framework.orchestrations import SequentialBuilder

storage = InMemoryCheckpointStorage()
workflow = SequentialBuilder(
    participants=[agent1, agent2],
    checkpoint_storage=storage
).build()
What gets saved: agent state, message history, current step. Good for development, not for production.
❓ Why this matters
Without checkpoints, a crash in step 5 of a 10-step workflow means restarting from step 1. Wasted time and LLM tokens.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF auto-saves at superstep boundaries. InMemoryCheckpointStorage is zero-config for development.
🔀 Alternatives
Manual state serialization, database snapshots. MAF's checkpoint API is automatic and transparent.
2 Complete HITL flow expand
🧪 Example
request_info mechanism: 
# Workflow level: node pauses, waits for human input
answer = await request_info("Should I proceed?")
if answer == "yes":
    # continue
    ...

# Tool level: approval mode
@tool(approval_mode="always_require")
def publish_article(content: str):
    """Publish article (requires approval)"""
    ...
Two levels: tool-level (approval_mode) and workflow-level (request_info). Both pause execution and wait for human input.
❓ Why this matters
Fully autonomous agents are dangerous for high-stakes tasks. HITL adds a safety net at critical decision points.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF supports HITL at two levels: tool-level (approval_mode) and workflow-level (request_info). Both pause execution and wait for human input.
🔀 Alternatives
External approval queues (Slack bots, email), polling-based checks. MAF's HITL is built into the execution model.
3 DurableTask configuration expand
🧪 Example
Install and setup: 
pip install agent-framework-durabletask

# Integration with Azure Functions
# See packages/durabletask for configuration details
Durability guarantees: state survives process restarts, supports long waits (hours/days).
❓ Why this matters
In-memory checkpoints die with the process. Production needs state that survives restarts, deployments, and infrastructure failures.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's DurableTask package leverages the battle-tested Durable Task Framework. Pair with Azure Functions for serverless, auto-scaling deployment.
🔀 Alternatives
Temporal, AWS Step Functions, Prefect. DurableTask integrates natively with Azure ecosystem.
4 File / Cosmos persistent storage expand
🧪 Example
Switch from InMemoryCheckpointStorage to Cosmos (or local FileCheckpointStorage): 
# Install package (cloud distributed backend)
pip install agent-framework-azure-cosmos

# Change one line of config: use a persistent backend
from agent_framework_azure_cosmos import CosmosCheckpointStorage
storage = CosmosCheckpointStorage(
    endpoint="https://...",
    database_name="agents",
    container_name="checkpoints",
    credential="...",
)

# Workflow build stays the same
workflow = SequentialBuilder(
    participants=[agent1, agent2],
    checkpoint_storage=storage
).build()
Switching backends is a one-line change. All checkpoint APIs stay the same.
❓ Why this matters
In-memory storage is fast but volatile. FileCheckpointStorage gives zero-dependency local persistence; CosmosCheckpointStorage gives global distribution + strong consistency. (The Redis package provides context / history memory, not workflow checkpoints.)
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's storage abstraction means switching backends is a one-line change. All checkpoint APIs stay the same.
🔀 Alternatives
PostgreSQL, DynamoDB, S3. For checkpoints, MAF core ships File and agent-framework-azure-cosmos ships Cosmos; the Redis package serves the context / history memory axis. The community can add more backends.
✅ Key points
  • Checkpoints: auto-saved at Workflow supersteps; crash-recoverable.
  • HITL: tool approval_mode + workflow request_info.
  • Durability: DurableTask package persists state externally for cross-process resume.
💡 Design highlight
Production features aren't plugins — they're part of the framework: checkpoints, approvals and durability are in the core API, not third-party libraries you glue on yourself.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. Where does a Workflow auto-save checkpoints, and what's the point?
  1. Never automatically; you must call save() by hand
  2. After every single token is generated
  3. Only once, after workflow.run returns
  4. At every superstep boundary; after a crash it resumes from the latest save instead of restarting from scratch
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. A superstep is the workflow's batch boundary - a natural consistency snapshot point. A save holds Agent state + message history + current step; a 10-step run that dies at step 5 resumes from step 5, saving time and LLM tokens.
2. Regarding the persistent backend for checkpoints, which statement is correct?
  1. Workflow checkpoints use FileCheckpointStorage (core) or CosmosCheckpointStorage (azure-cosmos package); the Redis package is for context / history memory, not a checkpoint backend
  2. There's only an in-memory backend; persistence is impossible
  3. Redis is the default checkpoint backend
  4. Switching backends requires rewriting the whole workflow
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. Reality: core ships InMemory / File; agent-framework-azure-cosmos ships Cosmos. The Redis package exports RedisContextProvider / RedisHistoryProvider (memory / history) - a different durability axis. Switching backends is a one-line change to constructing storage; the checkpoint_storage= interface stays the same.
3. At which two levels does MAF's human-in-the-loop (HITL) offer pause points?
  1. By polling with time.sleep
  2. Only the tool level
  3. Only a UI-level popup
  4. Tool level approval_mode="always_require" + workflow level request_info
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. Two routes: require approval before a single tool call (approval_mode), or have a workflow node request_info to pause, ask and await a reply. Both make "waiting on a human" a first-class state - combined with checkpointing, the pending approval can even survive a crash-and-resume.

  • A 10-step workflow with one human approval crashes at step 6. Using checkpointing + persistent backend + HITL, explain how it recovers to the correct state on restart, whether that approval result is lost, and whether you'd pick the File or Cosmos backend and why.
Part 5 · Build Your Own

22 · Capstone: A Multi-Agent Workflow

Capstone: assemble Agent + tools + orchestration + checkpoints from earlier lessons into a multi-Agent workflow.

🧩 Analogy
This lesson is like building with LEGO: earlier lessons taught you each brick type (Agent, tools, orchestration, checkpoints); now you snap them together into a complete car — one that drives, turns and brakes.

Scenario: write → review

1

Create two Agents

Writer: writes an article from a prompt. Reviewer: reviews it, suggests edits.

2

Orchestrate with SequentialBuilder

Writer finishes → Reviewer reviews, in sequence.

3

Run it

workflow.run("Write about AI agents").

end-to-end skeleton
from agent_framework import Agent
from agent_framework.orchestrations import SequentialBuilder

writer = Agent(client=client, name="Writer",
    instructions="Write a short article on the given topic.")
reviewer = Agent(client=client, name="Reviewer",
    instructions="Review the article. Suggest improvements.")

workflow = SequentialBuilder(participants=[writer, reviewer]).build()
result = await workflow.run("Write about AI agents")

How the bricks snap together

Provider
OpenAIChatClient
→
Writer
+ tools + middleware
→
Reviewer
review Agent
→
SequentialBuilder
chain into a workflow
→
+ checkpoint
checkpoint_storage
→
workflow.run
end to end

Every arrow maps to an earlier lesson: Provider (L16) supplies the model, tools / middleware (L06 / L18) enrich a single Agent, the Builder (L12-13) orchestrates multiple Agents, and checkpointing (L19) makes it recoverable. The capstone's value is seeing how these pieces compose orthogonally - each independent, yet snapping together.

1 Extended code skeleton expand
🧪 Example
Full working example in ~25-30 lines: 
from agent_framework import Agent, tool
from agent_framework.openai import OpenAIChatClient
from agent_framework.orchestrations import SequentialBuilder
from agent_framework import InMemoryCheckpointStorage

# Tool
@tool
def web_search(query: str) -> str:
    """Search the web"""
    return f"Results for {query}"

# Middleware
class LoggingMiddleware(ChatMiddleware):
    async def process(self, context, call_next):
        logger.info(f"Input: {context.messages}")
        await call_next()
        logger.info(f"Output: {context.result}")

# Agents
client = OpenAIChatClient(model="gpt-4o")
writer = client.as_agent(name="Writer",
    instructions="Write articles",
    tools=[web_search],
    middleware=[LoggingMiddleware()])

reviewer = client.as_agent(name="Reviewer",
    instructions="Review and improve")

# Workflow
storage = InMemoryCheckpointStorage()
workflow = SequentialBuilder(
    participants=[writer, reviewer],
    checkpoint_storage=storage
).build()

result = await workflow.run("Write about MAF")
All pieces together: agent, tool, middleware, checkpoint, orchestration.
❓ Why this matters
Seeing all pieces together cements understanding. Copy-paste-able code is the best learning tool.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's composability means each piece (agent, tool, middleware, checkpoint, orchestration) snaps together cleanly.
🔀 Alternatives
Boilerplate-heavy frameworks need 100+ lines for the same. MAF keeps it under 30.
2 Adding concurrency expand
🧪 Example
Replace Sequential with: Writer + FactChecker run in parallel, then Reviewer runs serially: 
from agent_framework.orchestrations import (
    SequentialBuilder, ConcurrentBuilder
)

# Stage 1: concurrent
concurrent_stage = ConcurrentBuilder(
    participants=[writer, fact_checker]
).build()

# Stage 2: sequential (waits for concurrent stage)
workflow = SequentialBuilder(
    participants=[concurrent_stage, reviewer]
).build()

result = await workflow.run("Write about AI")
Builders compose: mix parallel and sequential stages.
❓ Why this matters
Sequential workflows are slow when steps are independent. Parallel execution cuts wall-clock time.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's ConcurrentBuilder + SequentialBuilder compose naturally. Mix parallel and sequential stages freely.
🔀 Alternatives
Manual asyncio.gather(), thread pools. MAF's builders handle fan-out/fan-in automatically.
3 Adding HITL expand
🧪 Example
Add a publish tool to Reviewer with approval_mode="always_require": 
@tool(approval_mode="always_require")
def publish(content: str):
    """Publish article (requires approval)"""
    # actual publish logic
    return f"Published: {content[:50]}..."

reviewer = client.as_agent(
    name="Reviewer",
    instructions="Review and publish if good",
    tools=[publish]  # must be human-approved before publish
)
Before the article is published, a human must approve. Framework handles pausing, notifying, and resuming.
❓ Why this matters
Auto-publishing without review is risky. HITL at the publish step prevents bad content from going live.
✅ How MAF does it
One parameter (approval_mode) on the tool definition. The framework handles pausing, notifying, and resuming.
🔀 Alternatives
Custom webhook approval, external moderation API. MAF's approach is declarative and built-in.
4 Production checklist expand
🧪 Example
Production-ready checklist: 
# ✓ Add OpenTelemetry tracing
from agent_framework.observability import configure_otel_providers
configure_otel_providers()

# ✓ Switch to persistent storage
from agent_framework import FileCheckpointStorage
storage = FileCheckpointStorage("./checkpoints")

# ✓ Add error handling middleware
middleware=[ErrorHandlingMiddleware(), RetryMiddleware()]

# ✓ Set token budgets
agent = client.as_agent(..., max_tokens=10000)

# ✓ Add rate limiting
# ✓ Configure logging
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)
Checklist of gaps from demo to production.
❓ Why this matters
Demo code ≠ production code. Knowing the gaps prevents outages and cost overruns.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF provides all production building blocks: OTel integration, persistent storage packages, middleware for retries/logging/billing. No third-party glue needed.
🔀 Alternatives
Platform-specific solutions (Azure Monitor, Datadog). MAF is cloud-agnostic; OTel exports to any backend.
✅ Key points
  • Multi-Agent workflow = a few Agents + one Builder + .build().run().
  • Tools, middleware, checkpoints, HITL — all stackable.
  • From "hello agent" to "production multi-Agent" with minimal code increase.
💡 Design highlight
Complexity is incremental: single Agent → add tools → add orchestration → add checkpoints → add approvals, each step adds only a few lines. That's the "prototype to production" experience.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. The capstone assembles provider / tools / middleware / orchestration / checkpointing. Which MAF design does that best illustrate?
  1. All the features are really one giant class
  2. These capabilities are orthogonal: independent with stable interfaces, so they compose like LEGO without changing each other's code
  3. They must be wired in a fixed order; swap one and everything breaks
  4. Composition only works in the enterprise edition
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. Each brick is exposed via a stable interface (ChatClient, @tool, middleware=, *Builder, checkpoint_storage=). Orthogonal means composable: adding middleware doesn't affect orchestration, swapping providers doesn't affect tools. The capstone makes that visible.
2. In the writer → reviewer SequentialBuilder workflow, how does checkpointing get wired in?
  1. Manually save inside each Agent's run()
  2. Checkpointing must be wrapped in a separate with block
  3. Pass storage to SequentialBuilder(participants=[…], checkpoint_storage=storage); the workflow auto-saves at superstep boundaries
  4. Sequential workflows don't support checkpointing
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. Checkpointing is a Builder parameter; the orchestration layer owns saving, and the Agent need not know about persistence. That's layered composition: the Agent "thinks", the Builder handles "orchestration + saving" - separate concerns that don't leak into each other.
