Part 6 · Protocols & Ecosystem

28 · Evaluation & Time-Travel

Evaluation systematically tests Agent output quality; Time-travel replays workflow runs from checkpoints for debugging — together they take Agents from "it runs" to "it's reliable".

📚 Analogy
Evaluation is grading an exam — give the Agent a set of questions, score the answers with a rubric. Time-travel is instant replay in sports — rewind to any moment and analyse frame-by-frame what went wrong.

Core evaluation concepts

ConceptRoleSource
EvalItemOne eval datum = input + expected output_evaluation.py
EvaluatorEvaluator protocol (Azure Foundry / local LLM-as-judge / custom)_evaluation.py
RubricScoreMulti-dimensional score (e.g. relevance, accuracy, completeness)_evaluation.py
EvalResultsResult summary (pass rate, per-item scores, report URL)_evaluation.py

Evaluation workflow

evaluate an Agent
from agent_framework import (
    Agent, LocalEvaluator, evaluate_agent, evaluator, keyword_check
)

# Custom evaluator function
@evaluator
def is_helpful(response: str) -> bool:
    return len(response) > 10

# Combine built-in + custom checks
local = LocalEvaluator(keyword_check("weather"), is_helpful)

# Run evaluation
results = await evaluate_agent(
    agent=agent,
    queries=["What's the weather?", "Will it rain?"],
    evaluators=local,
)
results[0].raise_for_status()  # assert pass in CI

🧪 Worked example: how evaluation blocks a "regression" in CI

The real value of evaluation isn't "producing a score" — it's automatically catching quality drops when prompts/models change. Scenario: you change the Agent's instructions to "answer more concisely", and it quietly drops the key phrase "refund policy". The naked eye won't catch it; an eval set will:

1

Freeze a baseline

Pin a set of queries plus expectations (keyword_check("refund policy"):1062 / expected_output) into an eval set — these are the Agent's "test cases".

2

Make a change

Edit the instructions, swap the model, or tweak a tool. Any one of these can silently shift output quality.

3

Re-run in one line

evaluate_agent(agent=, queries=, evaluators=) (_evaluation.py:1629) calls agent.run() per query, converts each interaction into an EvalItem (:182), then hands them to Evaluator.evaluate(items, *, eval_name) (:705) for scoring.

4

Compare results

It returns list[EvalResults] (:373). This time r.passed/r.total (:441/:451) drops from 2/2 to 1/2, and that item's status becomes "failed" — the key phrase is gone.

5

Gate CI

results[0].raise_for_status() (:470) raises → the pipeline turns red → the regression is blocked before merge. Evaluation goes from "manual spot-check" to "automated guardrail".

queries
test inputs
→
agent.run()
run each
→
EvalItem
input+response+expected
→
Evaluator.evaluate
score
→
EvalResults
passed/total

evaluate_agent is all keyword-only (after the *), with evaluators= the only required argument; pass responses= and it skips running the Agent and scores existing responses directly. One pipeline compresses "batch-run + score + assert" into a single call.

🧪 Worked example: how time-travel replays from a breakpoint

Second scenario: a 5-step workflow fails at step 4 due to a network blip. Without checkpoints you'd rerun all 4 steps — wasting time and tokens. With checkpoint storage, each superstep dropped a WorkflowCheckpoint, chained via previous_checkpoint_id into a reversible timeline:

1

Enable checkpointing

Pass WorkflowBuilder(checkpoint_storage=storage) (_workflow_builder.py:96). Note: it's a constructor argument, not a with_checkpointing() method. After each superstep the framework auto-saves (:122) a checkpoint.

2

Chain into a timeline

Every WorkflowCheckpoint (_checkpoint.py:31) carries a previous_checkpoint_id (:75) pointing at the prior one — that chain is the timeline, rewindable to any frame. It also stores workflow_name / graph_signature_hash / iteration_count (:71/:72/:84).

3

The failure

Step 4 throws; execution halts, but the state of steps 1–3 has already been persisted as cp1/cp2/cp3 — nothing is lost.

4

Fetch a checkpoint

cp = await storage.get_latest(workflow_name=wf.name) (:169), or grab any exact frame by id with load(checkpoint_id) (:133); list_checkpoints (:147) enumerates the whole timeline.

5

Replay and resume

await wf.run(checkpoint_id=cp.checkpoint_id, checkpoint_storage=storage) (_workflow.py:681) → internally restore_from_checkpoint (:660). Completed steps are skipped; only the breakpoint onward reruns; graph_signature_hash first validates the topology still matches, preventing replay into a changed graph.

cp1
after step1
→
cp2
after step2
→
cp3
after step3 · replay here
→
step4…
rerun only the unfinished

Direction of the chain: the arrows show happen-order cp1 → cp2 → cp3, while previous_checkpoint_id is the back-pointer (cp3.previous = cp2). Two storage impls: InMemoryCheckpointStorage (:192, for tests) and FileCheckpointStorage (:239, persisted across processes).

🔍 Real source: the eval entry point and the checkpoint structure

_evaluation.py / _checkpoint.pyeval entry + checkpoint timeline (verified verbatim)
# _evaluation.py:1629 — the simplest dev-time eval entry (all keyword args)
async def evaluate_agent(*, agent=None, queries=None, expected_output=None,
        expected_tool_calls=None, responses=None,
        evaluators, eval_name=None, ...) -> list[EvalResults]: ...

class Evaluator(Protocol):                              # :683
    async def evaluate(self, items: Sequence[EvalItem], *,
                       eval_name: str) -> EvalResults: ...   # :705

# _workflows/_checkpoint.py:31 — one frame = a frozen point on a timeline
@dataclass
class WorkflowCheckpoint:
    workflow_name: str                                   # :71
    graph_signature_hash: str                            # :72 validates topology
    checkpoint_id: CheckpointID                          # :74
    previous_checkpoint_id: CheckpointID | None = None     # :75 chain = timeline
    iteration_count: int = 0                             # :84

class CheckpointStorage(Protocol):                       # :119
    async def save(self, cp) -> CheckpointID: ...           # :122
    async def load(self, checkpoint_id) -> WorkflowCheckpoint: ...  # :133
    async def get_latest(self, *, workflow_name): ...       # :169

# replay = resume from a frame (_workflow.py:681)
await wf.run(checkpoint_id=cp.checkpoint_id, checkpoint_storage=storage)

Read the two side by side and the commonality between "evaluation" and "time-travel" is clear: both turn one execution into inspectable, replayable data. Evaluation freezes the interaction into an EvalItem handed to a scoring protocol; time-travel freezes the workflow state into a WorkflowCheckpoint chained on a timeline. Both define swappable backends via a Protocol (cloud/local evaluators, in-memory/file storage).

Why "evaluation + replay" make a pair

They share a lesson because they complete the two halves of the "make the Agent reliable" loop:

Loop stageOwnerCapability
Detect the problemevaluate_agentbatch-run + score; raise_for_status() blocks regressions in CI
Diagnose the causewf.run(checkpoint_id=)replay from the failing frame, step through each Executor's I/O
Fix & verifyboth togetherre-run evaluate_agent; the score recovering = the fix worked
Audit & reproduceWorkflowCheckpointa checkpoint is a deterministic snapshot; graph_signature_hash ensures replay only into a matching graph

Without evaluation, after editing a prompt you can only "feel like it got better"; without time-travel, evaluation tells you "query 3 failed" but not which step broke. Together, the Agent truly moves from "it runs" to "reliable, regression-proof, auditable". Alongside the capabilities of L23–L26 (skills, tools, hosting, interconnect), they form the complete toolbox for pushing an Agent into production.

1 Evaluation workflow expand
🧪 Example
The full evaluation flow: 
# 1. Define eval data
queries = ["What's the weather?", "Will it rain?"]

# 2. Run Agent to get responses
# evaluate_agent() calls agent.run() for each query

# 3. Score
# Each Evaluator scores every (input, output) pair

# 4. Inspect results
for r in results:
    print(f"{r.provider}: {r.passed}/{r.total}")
    for item in r.items:
        for score in item.scores:
            print(f"  {score.name}: {'PASS' if score.passed else 'FAIL'}")
❓ Why this matters
LLM output is non-deterministic — the same question may get a different answer each time. Without systematic evaluation you can't tell whether a prompt change made the Agent better or worse. Evaluation is the Agent's unit test suite.
✅ How MAF does it
evaluate_agent() runs the full pipeline in one call: auto-invokes agent.run() for each query, collects responses, hands them to Evaluators. Supports multiple backends: Azure AI Foundry cloud eval, LocalEvaluator for local checks, custom @evaluator functions.
🔀 Alternatives
Manual testing (not repeatable, not scalable); OpenAI Evals (OpenAI-specific); RAGAS (focused on RAG evaluation); LangSmith (LangChain-ecosystem tracing + eval).
2 RubricScore multi-dimensional scoring expand
🧪 Example
RubricScore breaks evaluation into dimensions: 
# EvalScoreResult.dimensions contains:
#   RubricScore(name="relevance", score=0.9)
#   RubricScore(name="accuracy", score=0.85)
#   RubricScore(name="completeness", score=0.7)

# EvalScoreResult also has:
#   name: evaluator name
#   score: aggregate score
#   passed: whether threshold was met
#   sample: raw evaluator output (rationale)
❓ Why this matters
A single aggregate score is not enough — an Agent response may be "relevant but inaccurate" or "accurate but incomplete". Multi-dimensional scoring pinpoints the Agent's weak spots for targeted improvement.
✅ How MAF does it
EvalScoreResult contains aggregate score + dimension scores + raw rationale (sample). AgentEvalConverter auto-converts Agent I/O to eval format. Results support raise_for_status() for CI gating.
🔀 Alternatives
Single-dimension LLM-as-judge (simple but low information); Human annotation (most accurate but costly and slow); Custom scorer functions (flexible but more code — MAF's @evaluator is the streamlined version).
3 Time-travel mechanics expand
🧪 Example
Replay a workflow from a checkpoint: 
from agent_framework import InMemoryCheckpointStorage

storage = InMemoryCheckpointStorage()
workflow = builder.build()

# First run — checkpoints are saved
async for event in workflow.run(message=10, stream=True):
    if should_interrupt(): break

# Resume from checkpoint — skip completed steps
cp = await storage.get_latest(workflow_name=workflow.name)
async for event in workflow.run(
    checkpoint_id=cp.checkpoint_id, stream=True
):
    # Completed @step functions return cached results
    # Only pending steps actually execute
❓ Why this matters
Workflows can halt mid-way due to network failures, timeouts or deliberate interrupts. Without checkpoints you'd rerun everything from scratch (wasting time and token budget). Time-travel lets you resume from where you left off — completed steps use cached results.
✅ How MAF does it
WorkflowCheckpoint captures full state: message history, Executor states, iteration count and pending HITL events. CheckpointStorage protocol has in-memory and file implementations. Executors save custom state via on_checkpoint_save/restore hooks.
🔀 Alternatives
Durable Functions (Azure's durable execution — lower level); Temporal.io (dedicated workflow engine — heavier); Manual intermediate state (works but easy to miss edge cases).
4 Combining eval + replay expand
🧪 Example
Evaluation and time-travel work hand-in-hand: 
# 1. Run eval — spot a failing query
results = await evaluate_agent(agent=agent, queries=[...])

# 2. Time-travel the failing workflow
# Replay from checkpoint, inspect each @step's I/O

# 3. Fix and re-evaluate to confirm improvement
# results[0].raise_for_status() passes = fix confirmed
❓ Why this matters
Evaluation spots the problem; time-travel locates the cause — a complete detect → diagnose → fix → verify loop. Without time-travel you know "this query failed" but not which step went wrong.
✅ How MAF does it
Evaluation and replay share the same Agent / Workflow abstractions — eval runs agent.run(); replay runs workflow.run(checkpoint_id=...). In HITL scenarios _set_responses pre-fills human answers so replay is fully automated.
🔀 Alternatives
Log analysis only (less complete than full checkpoint state); LangSmith Trace (visual tracing but can't resume execution from a breakpoint); Unit-test mocking (fast but doesn't test real LLM behaviour).
✅ Key points
  • Evaluation = Agent unit tests: EvalItem (input) → Evaluator (score) → EvalResults (results).
  • RubricScore supports multi-dimensional scoring; raise_for_status() for CI gating.
  • Time-travel via WorkflowCheckpoint saves/restores full state; completed steps use cache.
  • Together they form a complete "detect → diagnose → fix → verify" loop.
💡 Design highlight
Evaluation and replay share the same Agent/Workflow abstractions — no extra debugging tools or tracing systems needed. One evaluate_agent() call = batch Agent runs + auto scoring + CI integration. One checkpoint_id parameter = resume from any breakpoint, diagnosing issues step by step.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. In CI, what does the line results[0].raise_for_status() after evaluate_agent(...) do?
  1. Uploads the eval results to the production database
  2. Raises when scores fall short, turning the pipeline red — blocking a quality regression before merge
  3. Raises the model temperature for better scores
  4. Auto-reruns every failed query until it passes
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. evaluate_agent (_evaluation.py:1629) calls agent.run() per query, converts each into an EvalItem, scores via Evaluator.evaluate, and returns list[EvalResults]. raise_for_status() (:470) mirrors the requests method of the same name: it raises when scores fall short. Dropped into CI, it turns evaluation from a "manual spot-check" into an "automated guardrail" — when a prompt/model change drops passed/total (:441/:451), the merge is blocked automatically.
2. Which field of WorkflowCheckpoint chains a series of checkpoints into a rewindable "timeline"?
  1. previous_checkpoint_id — a back-pointer to the prior frame; the chain is the timeline
  2. workflow_name — it's just a name
  3. iteration_count — it's just an incrementing counter
  4. graph_signature_hash — it only validates topology
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. On WorkflowCheckpoint (_checkpoint.py:31), previous_checkpoint_id (:75) points at the prior checkpoint, forming a chain rewindable to any frame — the timeline. graph_signature_hash (:72) validates topology before replay, iteration_count (:84) records the iteration, and workflow_name (:71) identifies ownership — all matter, but the "timeline" comes from the previous_checkpoint_id chain.
3. A 5-step workflow fails at step 4. With checkpointing enabled, what is the cheapest way to recover?
  1. Rerun all 5 steps from scratch with wf.run(message=...)
  2. Manually copy-paste each step's intermediate result back in
  3. wf.run(checkpoint_id=cp3, checkpoint_storage=storage) — replay from the breakpoint frame; completed steps are skipped
  4. Delete the checkpoints and rebuild the workflow
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. Passing checkpoint_id (optionally with checkpoint_storage) to wf.run() (_workflow.py:681) triggers the internal restore_from_checkpoint (:660): it restores full state from that frame, skips completed steps, and resumes only from the breakpoint — saving time and tokens. Fetch checkpoints via get_latest(workflow_name=) (:169) or by id with load (:133); storage comes as InMemoryCheckpointStorage (:192) or FileCheckpointStorage (:239).
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Your Agent regresses: "query 3" drops in score, and behind it sits a multi-step workflow. (1) In the "detect → diagnose → fix → verify" loop, which stage does evaluation (evaluate_agent) own versus time-travel (wf.run(checkpoint_id=))? (2) Why is a checkpoint a "deterministic snapshot", and what does graph_signature_hash protect during replay? (3) If you change the workflow's graph structure and then replay an old checkpoint, what happens and why is that a good thing?
Part 7 · Bonus

29 · AF vs Other Frameworks

MAF isn't the only agent framework. This lesson compares four major ones to give you global coordinates.

🗺️ Analogy
MAF is a versatile SUV (city to off-road), LangGraph is a manual-shift sports car (precise control), AutoGen is an experimental off-roader (trail blazer), SK is a business sedan (enterprise smooth).

Four-framework comparison

MAFLangGraphAutoGenSemantic Kernel
OrchestrationGraph (Workflow) + prebuilt patternsGraph (StateGraph)Multi-agent chatPlanner + pipeline
LanguagesPython + .NETPython (+ JS)Python + .NETPython + .NET + Java
ProductionCheckpoints · HITL · OTel · DurableTaskCheckpoints · HITL · LangSmithBasic loggingConnectors · enterprise
PositioningPrototype-to-prod, SK + AutoGen successorGraph control flow + controllable agentsMulti-agent researchEnterprise AI orchestration

👇 Expand the cards below for deep dives — code examples, architecture differences, selection guidance.

1 MAF vs LangGraph deep dive expand
🧪 Example
The same task — "two agents in sequence" — in both frameworks: 
# MAF: SequentialBuilder
from agent_framework.orchestrations import SequentialBuilder
wf = SequentialBuilder(participants=[writer, reviewer]).build()
result = await wf.run("Write a poem")

# LangGraph: hand-draw StateGraph
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph
graph = StateGraph(State)
graph.add_node("writer", writer_fn)
graph.add_node("reviewer", reviewer_fn)
graph.add_edge("writer", "reviewer")
app = graph.compile()
result = app.invoke({"input": "Write a poem"})
❓ Why this matters
LangGraph is the graph orchestration layer in the LangChain ecosystem — large user base, rich docs. When evaluating tech, these two get compared constantly. You need to know their essential difference: the level of orchestration abstraction, not just "both are graphs".
✅ How MAF does it
MAF provides two abstraction levels: low-level WorkflowBuilder (same tier as LangGraph's StateGraph — hand-draw the graph), and high-level prebuilt orchestrations (Sequential / Concurrent / Handoff / Group / Magentic). Most scenarios use the prebuilts in a few lines; complex topologies drop down to the manual graph. LangGraph only has the manual graph — even simple tasks require drawing. MAF is also natively dual-language (Python + .NET); LangGraph is mainly Python.
🔀 Alternatives
LangGraph suits teams already in the LangChain ecosystem needing the finest graph control. Mixing both is viable — use MAF's Agent + ChatClient but LangGraph for orchestration (they don't conflict). Temporal / Prefect (general workflow engines) can also orchestrate agents but lack AI-native abstractions.
2 MAF vs AutoGen deep dive expand
🧪 Example
AutoGen's paradigm: "multiple agents chatting in a group": 
# AutoGen style (in MAF via MagenticBuilder)
from agent_framework.orchestrations import MagenticBuilder
wf = MagenticBuilder(
    participants=[coder, reviewer, tester],
    manager=manager,
).build()
result = await wf.run("Build a calculator app")
The original AutoGen requires assembling GroupChat + GroupChatManager yourself; MAF packages this as MagenticBuilder.
❓ Why this matters
AutoGen pioneered the "multi-agent conversation" paradigm (from Microsoft Research). MAF directly inherits AutoGen's Magentic-One idea (conductor + workers). Understanding the relationship helps you decide: "Is MAF enough, or do I still need original AutoGen?"
✅ How MAF does it
MAF makes AutoGen's multi-agent chat just one orchestration pattern among many, not the only paradigm. You can mix Sequential (simple chains) and Magentic (conductor dispatch) in one project. In AutoGen everything is "conversation" — even simple tasks force group chat. MAF's production features (checkpoints, OTel, DurableTask) also far exceed AutoGen's.
🔀 Alternatives
Original AutoGen still suits pure research / rapid prototyping (simpler API, active community). CrewAI is another multi-agent framework with more "human-like" role definitions but weaker production features. If you only need "two agents chatting", a hand-written loop is actually simplest.
3 MAF vs Semantic Kernel deep dive expand
🧪 Example
SK's core abstractions: Kernel + Plugin + Planner: 
# Semantic Kernel style
kernel = Kernel()
kernel.add_plugin(WeatherPlugin(), "weather")
result = await kernel.invoke_prompt("What's the weather in Seattle?")

# MAF — same thing
agent = Agent(client=client, tools=[get_weather],
    instructions="Answer weather questions.")
result = await agent.run("What's the weather in Seattle?")
❓ Why this matters
Semantic Kernel is one of MAF's predecessors — many enterprises already use it. Understanding SK → MAF evolution helps existing SK users decide whether and how to migrate.
✅ How MAF does it
SK excels at enterprise connectors (dozens of ready-made Plugins) and Planners (LLM auto-plans steps). MAF inherits these and adds: graph Workflow orchestration (SK doesn't have this), multi-agent collaboration (SK doesn't have this), DurableTask persistence, and a unified ChatClient abstraction (SK's ChatCompletionService is more fragmented). MAF is Microsoft's official "next-gen" direction.
🔀 Alternatives
Stay on SK — if you have many SK Plugins and don't need multi-agent / workflows, no rush. Gradual migration — SK and MAF can coexist; use MAF for new features, keep old ones on SK. Go MAF — for new projects, recommended since SK's maintenance focus has shifted to MAF.
4 Ecosystem & community comparison expand
🧪 Example
Rough picture as of mid-2026: 
# GitHub stars (order of magnitude)
MAF:       ~10k  (fast growth, full Microsoft backing)
LangChain: ~100k (largest community; LangGraph stars counted separately)
AutoGen:   ~40k  (active research community)
SK:        ~25k  (large enterprise user base)
❓ Why this matters
Community size affects: can you find answers when stuck, are there third-party plugins, do candidates know the framework when you're hiring. Not a technical issue, but often the deciding factor in practice.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF is young but backed by full Microsoft investment: official docs on MS Learn, Discord community, deep Azure AI Foundry integration. Enterprise users naturally trust Microsoft backing. MAF also absorbed users from both SK and AutoGen communities — steep growth curve.
🔀 Alternatives
If community and ecosystem richness is your top priority, LangChain is still the largest. If academic frontier matters most, AutoGen has the most paper output. If enterprise stability + Microsoft ecosystem, MAF or SK. There's no "best" — only "best for your scenario".

A one-glance selection table

If you value most…Better fitWhy
Prototype-to-production in one stack (checkpoints / HITL / OTel)MAFProduction features are first-class; prebuilt orchestrations + manual graph cover both ends
The finest-grained graph control flowLangGraphStateGraph lets you hand-draw every node and edge for maximum precision
Multi-agent chat / academic explorationAutoGenBirthplace of the "multi-agent group chat" paradigm, active research community
An existing trove of enterprise connectors / pluginsSemantic KernelDozens of ready-made plugins, mature enterprise integration
Microsoft ecosystem + long-term directionMAFThe officially recommended successor unifying SK + AutoGen

Note: these are not mutually exclusive. MAF's Agent / ChatClient can pair with LangGraph's orchestration; SK and MAF can coexist for gradual migration. Picking a framework is choosing the "main line", not a black-and-white split.

✅ Key points
  • MAF = SK + AutoGen merged, aimed at "prototype to production".
  • LangGraph excels at graph control precision; AutoGen at multi-agent chat exploration.
  • Pick by your core needs: language, production features, control granularity, ecosystem.
  • MAF offers two abstraction levels (prebuilt orchestrations + manual graph), covering simple to complex.
💡 Design highlight
MAF unifies graph orchestration and multi-agent chat in one framework — you don't have to choose between LangGraph and AutoGen. Prebuilt orchestrations are sugar; the underlying Workflow is the engine. Mix freely.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. MAF offers both a low-level WorkflowBuilder (hand-drawn graph) and high-level prebuilt orchestrations (Sequential / Concurrent / …). Versus LangGraph's single graph level, what's the benefit of this "two-tier" design?
  1. It forces everyone onto graphs for a uniform mental model
  2. It makes the framework bigger and look more professional
  3. Because prebuilt orchestrations run faster than hand-drawn graphs
  4. Simple tasks need only a few lines of prebuilt orchestration; complex topologies drop down to the hand-drawn graph - you don't pay the "draw every edge" cost for simple cases
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. Layered abstractions let you pick granularity on demand: ~80% of cases are covered by prebuilt orchestrations (low cognitive load), and the rest drop to WorkflowBuilder. LangGraph has only the graph level, so even a simple sequence means hand-drawing nodes and edges.
2. Why is "use MAF directly for new projects" usually sound advice for many SK / AutoGen users?
  1. MAF is Microsoft's converged successor to SK and AutoGen, absorbing both (connectors / Planner + multi-agent), and maintenance focus has shifted to MAF
  2. Because SK and AutoGen have been deleted
  3. Because MAF is the only open-source one
  4. Because MAF is incompatible with all old code and forces a full rewrite
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. MAF inherits SK's enterprise connectors / Planner ideas and AutoGen's Magentic-One (manager + workers) multi-agent paradigm, plus graph orchestration / DurableTask / a unified ChatClient. It's positioned as the "next generation", so new projects can adopt it directly while old ones migrate gradually and coexist.
3. Regarding "MAF vs LangGraph", which statement is most accurate?
  1. Using MAF means you can't touch any LangChain ecosystem
  2. They aren't necessarily either/or: you can pair MAF's Agent / ChatClient with LangGraph's orchestration, because the orchestration layer is swappable
  3. LangGraph doesn't support checkpointing
  4. The two are really the same code underneath
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. Framework boundaries are layered - the Agent / ChatClient abstraction and the orchestration layer can be decoupled. Mixing is viable (MAF for Agents, LangGraph for graph orchestration). Picking is choosing a "main line", not a mutually exclusive split.
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Your team has a Semantic Kernel codebase and now needs a new feature requiring "multi-agent collaboration + checkpoint recovery". Based on this lesson, would you rewrite directly in MAF or run SK / MAF side by side for gradual migration? Give two concrete reasons (weighing production features against migration cost).
Part 7 · Bonus

30 · Full-Stack Map & Learning Path

Final lesson — zoom out: place MAF in the AI full-stack coordinate system and see what's worth learning next door.

🔭 Analogy
You've been remodelling one floor (L6, orchestration) of a building. Now ride the elevator up and down: the basement is the engine room (inference), the lobby is the front desk (application). Understanding the whole building tells you where to connect the plumbing and wiring.

AI full-stack layers

L7Application
Your product / UI / API gateway (Chainlit · Streamlit · FastAPI).
L6Agent Orchestration
← You are here (MAF / LangGraph / AutoGen / SK).
L5Model API / Inference
OpenAI API · Azure AI · vLLM · llama.cpp · Ollama.
L4Vector Search / RAG
Embeddings · pgvector · Qdrant · Azure AI Search.

How one request travels through the layers

user asks
L7 application
→
MAF orchestration
L6 ← you are here
→
RAG fetch context
L4 vector search
→
model inference
L5 OpenAI / vLLM
→
assemble reply
L6
→
render back
L7 → user

A single Q&A travels up and down several layers: your MAF orchestration (L6) sits in the middle - reaching up to the app, down to inference, and over to L4 for knowledge when needed. Reading this dataflow tells you which layer to "connect the plumbing" in: private models change L5, a knowledge base changes L4, a new front end changes L7 - while the Agent logic (L6) stays basically untouched.

Orchestration schools

SchoolRepresentativeCore idea
Chain/pipelineLangChain LCELFunction composition, A | B | C
GraphLangGraph / MAF WorkflowDirected graph + state
Multi-agent chatAutoGen / MAF Group ChatMessage passing between agents
PlannerSKLLM plans steps then executes
DeclarativeMAF Declarative / RivetConfig-driven

👇 Expand the cards to dive into each neighbouring layer — what to learn, which tools, when to learn it.

1 L5 Inference layer deep dive expand
🧪 Example
Three inference choices: 
# vLLM — high-throughput GPU inference
vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3-8B --port 8000

# llama.cpp — CPU / edge inference
./main -m model.gguf -p "Hello"

# Ollama — local one-click model
ollama run llama3
❓ Why this matters
MAF calls models via ChatClient, but where does the model come from? If you use OpenAI API you don't worry about this; but for private deployment (data security, cost control, low latency) you need the inference layer. vLLM for high-concurrency GPU, llama.cpp for edge/CPU, Ollama for local dev.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF doesn't lock you to any inference layer: OpenAIChatClient can point at any OpenAI-compatible endpoint (vLLM, llama.cpp server mode, Ollama all qualify). Develop with OpenAI API, deploy on private vLLM — zero Agent code changes.
🔀 Alternatives
TGI (HuggingFace inference server), TensorRT-LLM (NVIDIA optimised), Azure AI Foundry (managed inference, zero ops). If you don't want to run GPUs yourself, cloud APIs are simplest.
2 L4 Vector search / RAG deep dive expand
🧪 Example
Core vector search flow: 
# 1. Text → embedding vector
vector = embedding_model.encode("What is MAF?")

# 2. Store in vector DB
db.insert(vector, metadata={"source": "docs/readme.md"})

# 3. At query time, find most similar
results = db.search(query_vector, top_k=5)

# 4. Feed retrieved text to Agent as context
context = "\n".join(r.text for r in results)
await agent.run(f"Based on: {context}\nAnswer: {question}")
❓ Why this matters
LLMs don't know your private data. RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) splices relevant document chunks into the prompt so the model can "see" your knowledge base. Vector search is RAG's core engine — turns "semantic similarity" into a database query.
✅ How MAF does it
MAF integrates RAG through ContextProvider: packages/azure-ai-search wraps Azure AI Search (enterprise vector search); you can also write your own ContextProvider to plug in pgvector / Qdrant / Pinecone. The Agent doesn't need to know where knowledge comes from — ContextProvider auto-injects before run.
🔀 Alternatives
pgvector (PostgreSQL extension, familiar to SQL users), Qdrant (cloud-native, purpose-built for vectors), Chroma (lightweight embedded, good for local prototypes), Azure AI Search (enterprise-grade, MAF has a ready-made package).
3 L7 Application layer deep dive expand
🧪 Example
Expose an MAF Agent as a web endpoint: 
# FastAPI + MAF Agent
from fastapi import FastAPI
app = FastAPI()

@app.post("/chat")
async def chat(prompt: str):
    result = await agent.run(prompt)
    return {"response": str(result)}
❓ Why this matters
Agents can't just run in a terminal — they need to face users. The application layer owns UI, API, auth, rate limiting. Knowing how to wrap an Agent into an API / web app is the key step from "it runs" to "it's usable".
✅ How MAF does it
MAF's hosting packages provide multiple deployment paths: ASP.NET Core / Azure Functions (.NET), FastAPI / Azure Functions (Python), A2A protocol (Agent-to-Agent, exposing an Agent as a service for other Agents), Foundry Hosted Agents (zero-ops deploy to Azure).
🔀 Alternatives
Chainlit (one-liner chat UI, great for demos), Streamlit (rapid data apps + chat interface), Gradio (ML demo interfaces), Custom React/Vue frontend (most flexible but most work).
4 Three recommended learning paths expand
🧪 Path A: I want to build a production chatbot
This guide L01-07 (basics) → L16 (pick a provider) → L18-19 (middleware + checkpoints) → L7 application layer (FastAPI / Foundry hosting) → L4 vector search (Azure AI Search / pgvector for RAG). Focus: get it running, add RAG, add production features.
❓ Path B: I want to understand AI infrastructure
This guide L01-03 (big picture) → L08-14 (internals) → L5 inference (vLLM / llama.cpp) → L4 vector search (hnswlib / pgvector) → L15 (read MAF source). Focus: drill down from the application layer; understand what each layer does.
✅ Path C: I want to contribute to MAF
All 22 lessons (build complete understanding) → L15 (set up env + run tests) → start with good first issue → read AGENTS.md + CODING_STANDARD.md → submit a PR. Focus: understand first, then act; start small, then go big.
🔀 General advice
Don't try to learn every layer at once — start from your goal and expand one layer up or down. Building Agent apps? Master L6 (orchestration), then look at L4 (RAG) and L7 (application). Building infrastructure? Master L5 (inference), then look at L6 (orchestration). Learn by doing is always more efficient than "learn everything first".
✅ Key points
  • MAF sits at L6 — the Agent Orchestration layer.
  • Next layer down (L5 inference / L4 vector search) deepens AI infrastructure.
  • Next layer up (L7 application) delivers agents to users.
  • Pick a learning path based on your goal: build products / learn fundamentals / contribute code.
💡 Design highlight
The orchestration layer is the "glue layer" — it connects models, tools, storage and applications. Knowing the layers above and below is key to becoming an Agent expert. You don't need to master every layer — just know what it does and how it interfaces with yours.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. MAF's OpenAIChatClient can point at vLLM / a llama.cpp server / Ollama. What's the biggest benefit?
  1. Because they're actually the same model
  2. These backends automatically make the model smarter
  3. Develop against the cloud OpenAI API, switch to private vLLM in production, and the Agent code doesn't change a line - inference and orchestration are decoupled
  4. Because tool calling only works this way
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. As long as the endpoint speaks the OpenAI API, the ChatClient can connect. Decoupling L5 (inference) from L6 (orchestration) means private deployment and cost / latency tuning are just "swap L5", leaving your Agent logic untouched.
2. This lesson groups orchestration into schools: chain / graph / multi-agent chat / Planner / declarative. What makes MAF special?
  1. It unifies "graph" and "multi-agent chat" in one framework - prebuilt orchestrations are sugar, the underlying Workflow is the engine, and you can mix them
  2. It supports only the declarative school
  3. It invented the chain pipeline
  4. It requires each project to pick exactly one school
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. Most frameworks bet on one school (LangChain=chain, LangGraph=graph, AutoGen=chat, SK=Planner). MAF folds graph orchestration and multi-agent chat into one engine, letting you mix per scenario within a single project instead of stitching frameworks together.
3. You want an existing MAF Agent to "answer from the company knowledge base". By the full-stack layering, which layer mainly changes, and why does Agent logic stay largely untouched?
  1. You must split the Agent into several to add knowledge
  2. Change the CSS in the L7 application layer
  3. Change the GPU driver in the L5 inference layer
  4. Mainly L4 (vector search / RAG), feeding retrieved context into the L6 Agent; layering makes "adding knowledge" a matter of plugging in a layer, not refactoring
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: D. The full-stack layering maps each need to a layer: private model→L5, knowledge base→L4, new front end→L7. RAG fetches context at L4 and injects it into the L6 Agent, so the orchestration logic stays stable - exactly "know the floor plan to know where to connect the pipes".
💭 Open questions (no single right answer - just think or try)
  • Place the MAF skills from this course into the full-stack map: which layer do you mainly work in? For a product that is "privately deployed + backed by a company knowledge base + with a web chat UI", say what you'd do at each of L7 / L6 / L5 / L4, and why this layering lets a team work in parallel.
Part 8 · Quick Reference

31 · Glossary & Quick Reference

Across the previous 30 lessons we went from "what is an Agent" all the way to memory backends, observability, and the protocol ecosystem. This lesson teaches nothing new—it is the whole guide's quick-reference and map: every core term collected into one place, each with a one-line definition, a real source location, and its home lesson, plus a picture of how they depend on each other. When you get stuck, come back here and locate anything at a glance.

🗺️ Analogy
After finishing a long trail you get a map + legend: every place name (term) has coordinates (source location) and a trail segment it belongs to (home lesson), and the map also draws which path connects to which (concept dependencies). This lesson is that map—it doesn't re-walk the trail, it lets you find any single point again, any time.

How to read this reference

Every table has three columns: a one-line definition (what the term actually means), a source location (which file and line to grep in the real repo—every line number has been verified), and its home lesson (click in for the full walkthrough). Terms are grouped into six buckets by "where you'll meet them", from closest-to-user to most ops-facing.

① User layer: what you touch every day

TermOne-line definitionSource locationLesson
Agent / BaseAgentBundles "model + tools + instructions" into an object you can run(); BaseAgent is the abstract base, Agent the default impl_agents.py:314 / :1584L8
ChatClient / BaseChatClientThe uniform channel for talking to one model provider: send messages, get messages back. An Agent "speaks" through it_clients.py:217L9
MessageThe basic unit of a conversation, carrying a role and a list of contents_types.py:1672L4
ContentA content block inside a message (text / tool call / result / image…), built via Content.from_text() etc._types.py:455L4

Concept dependency map: who stands on whom

Read bottom-up: every upper concept stands on the one below. When an upper layer confuses you, dropping down one layer often unblocks it.

BedrockMessage · Content · Role
The atoms of everything: a model's input and output are messages, and messages carry Content (text / tool call / result). (L4)
ChannelChatClient
Sends messages to a model provider and gets messages back. An Agent talks through it. (L9)
CoreAgent + Tools + Middleware
The run loop: model decides to call a tool → execute → feed back → ask again, wrapped layer by layer in middleware. (L8 / L10 / L11)
OrchestrationWorkflow · Executor · Edge · orchestrators
Multiple Agents / steps form a graph, flowing along edges; the five ready-made orchestrators are pre-packaged graphs. (L12 / L13)
MemoryContextProvider · HistoryProvider
Inject context before a run, store history after; Redis / Mem0 / Cosmos are the backends. (L7 / L28)
EcosystemSkill · MCP · A2A / AG-UI · hosting
Outward-facing: standardized tools (MCP), Agent-to-Agent (A2A), frontends (AG-UI), cloud hosting (Foundry). (L23–L26)
OpsCheckpoint · HITL · Eval · OTel · DevUI
Make it reliable, debuggable, trustworthy: snapshot / human-review / evaluate / trace / visualize. (L19 / L27 / L29 / L30)

The minimal dependency chain of one run

Message in
user input
→
Agent.run
+ Middleware
→
ChatClient
ask model
→
Tool exec
FunctionCall / Result
→
Message out
AgentResponse

Reverse index: find a lesson from a question

I want to…Go to
understand what actually happens in one run()L3 · L8

✅ Key points
  • The whole framework is layered: Message/Content is bedrock, then ChatClient → Agent → orchestration → ecosystem → ops on top.
  • Every term greps to a real source location (line numbers in this table are verified)—when a concept is unclear, go read those lines.
  • Can't recall something? Use the reverse index: start from "what I want to do" and jump straight to the lesson.
  • This lesson is a map, not a tutorial—its value is the moment you come back to look something up.
💡 Design insight
Everything ultimately reduces to "messages in, messages out". Whether it's an Agent, a workflow, or the cross-machine A2A protocol, what flows underneath is always a Message. It's this single unified "data contract" that lets so many layers compose—which is also why, once you truly get the Message in L4, every lesson up to L27 gets easier.

🧪 Self-test - think about the design

1. Per this lesson's concept dependency map, which dependency direction is correct?
  1. ChatClient builds on the orchestrators (Sequential, etc.)
  2. Agent builds on ChatClient, and ChatClient builds on the Message / Content bedrock
  3. OpenTelemetry is the bedrock of every other concept
  4. Message builds on Workflow
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: B. The map reads bottom-up: Message/Content (bedrock) → ChatClient (channel) → Agent (core) → orchestration → ecosystem → ops. Upper layers stand on lower ones, so Agent depends on ChatClient and ChatClient on the message atoms; Workflow/OTel sit on top and can't be the bedrock.
2. You want to confirm which file holds the "cross-session memory" classes—which column of the reference gives you that directly?
  1. The "lesson" column—it only gives a lesson number, not a file
  2. The reference has no file info; you must dig through source yourself
  3. The "source location" column: _sessions.py:348 / :410 (ContextProvider / HistoryProvider)
  4. The "one-line definition" column
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: C. Each row has three columns: one-line definition (what), source location (where to grep, line numbers verified), and lesson (full walkthrough). For "which file/line", read the source-location column: memory lives in _sessions.py as ContextProvider (:348) and HistoryProvider (:410).
3. This lesson says "everything reduces to messages in, messages out". Which claim does that most directly support?
  1. Because all layers share the single Message data contract, Agents, workflows, and the A2A protocol can compose together
  2. Messages only appear in L4 and are unused afterward
  3. Each layer must use its own incompatible data structure
  4. Workflows pass function pointers, not messages
Show answer & explanation click to expand
Answer: A. The unified Message contract is the root of composability: as long as an upper layer honors "take messages, emit messages", it plugs into the layer below. That's why getting L4's Message makes every later lesson easier—it's the data spine running through the whole stack.
